Meridian unaware.* Facts, in order, the way a thing that would have to hold up someday gets written down. Then he paused, pen above the page. The case was not finished and the predator was still in the field and the next part belonged to Rennick and Corvin and Tamsin — but the room was found, and the room had been the whole problem, because the room was the one place in the corridor the thing could walk a girl to and not follow. He had stood in it for three hours. He wrote one line that was not a fact and would never go in evidence: *It never knew I was there.* He underlined it once, the way he underlined the things he wanted to find again, and closed the notebook.

[Beat 320]
He closed the notebook and walked through the checkpoint arc on the corner of 18th and felt the query go out, find nothing, and file him as a glitch, the way it always did. He went to find the phone booth on 19th to call the office and tell Rennick what came next. The city moved around him with its fuller soundtrack — load-temperature and latency-pressure and two million neuretic addresses talking to each other through the ambient substrate — and Voss walked through all of it without it, carrying what he needed in a notebook in his jacket pocket.

The case was not finished. The predator was still in the field. Celeste still had the ears and the channel and the eight months of a voice she had believed completely. The next part would be Rennick's and Corvin's and Tamsin's, and it would ask things of them he could not give. What he could give he had given: the room, the route. He put the notebook away and walked north into the morning rush, past the breakfast place starting its lunch, and let the crowd close around him.

## The Surfacing

[Beat 321]
The Surfacing

EP9 - ensemble - under-city staging room, Cermak sub-level - Day 5 of the Hartley case - procedure window: 22 minutes

The procedure window was twenty-two minutes. Analog had calculated that from the operator's case files — the standard Scatter sequence, end-to-end — and had walked it backward from the time he'd found the staging room to establish when they needed to be inside.

Nineteen minutes now. They had come down the flood-stair fast, and the way back out was the same route, no other exit. They were committed the moment they went through the door.

Rennick checked the count and put it away.

They went through the door.

[Beat 322]
Analog came up out of the flood-stair first, because he was the one who'd found it and because the Tributary couldn't see him coming. The staging room was 130 feet past the stair landing, down a corridor where the water had risen and dropped so many times it had left tide-rings on the concrete like the inside of a kettle. Each ring was a different decade's runoff — rust, algae, a chalk line of mineral salt at chest height where the last big flood had stopped. The under-city didn't smell like the street. It smelled of standing water and old sealer and, threaded under that, something electrical and wrong — the way a transformer smells when it's been pushed past its rating for a long time and nobody's come to look at it. He stopped at the landing and held up three fingers. He pointed at his own chest, then forward. The others stacked behind him in the dark.

[Beat 323]
Rennick had the count. He'd set it the moment Analog's paper map went on the table at 0515: the Scatter's procedure window was twenty-two minutes — the minimum continuous read-sequence the rig needed to complete a dissemination. Interrupt before fourteen minutes and you interrupted a partial. Interrupt after eighteen and you were collecting pieces. They were three minutes from the room and they'd burned four getting down the flood-stair without lights. Fifteen minutes left on the window, maybe less.

[Beat 324]
Tamsin put her hand flat on the corridor wall and kept it there for three seconds. The others waited. She pulled it back and touched Rennick's sleeve — two fingers. Two people in the room beyond. She held up a closed fist: the room itself was wrong. Not violence-wrong, which had a heat to it she knew by now, and not the cold dread of a snatch. This was a worn thing. The kind of residue a place got when the same event happened in it over and over with different people, each one walking in calm. She didn't have a word for it. She made herself stop reaching for one and moved up behind Analog.

[Beat 325]
Rennick leaned in close to Analog's ear and didn't bother whispering much. "The second person — contract?"

Analog nodded. He'd made Aleksei two blocks back, a gray-coat man standing in a doorway reading a physical magazine nobody had printed in fifteen years. Meridian's field operators had tells. Aleksei's was that he was the only person in the under-city who wasn't watching his footing on the wet concrete.

[Beat 326]
Rennick found Aleksei in the corridor outside the staging room door — not inside, not blocking. Standing at the margin of the thing, which was a choice. He stopped six feet out and kept his hands where Aleksei could see them.

"Your brief is organ op," Rennick said.

"Was," Aleksei said. He tucked the magazine into his coat. "I updated my report this morning. Insufficient evidence of target matching client criteria. Recommend case suspension pending further review." He looked at the door. "You have about twelve minutes."

[Beat 327]
Aleksei stepped aside and leaned against the corridor wall with the posture of someone who had discovered a personal interest in counting the ceiling tiles. Rennick gave him a single nod and didn't waste another second on him. The door was steel-framed composite, the lock a physical barrel — not a network lock, obviously — and Analog already had the tension wrench out. Twenty seconds.

[Beat 328]
The room was larger than Analog had expected from the exterior wall. Bare concrete, a bank of hardware along the left side — quantum-capable compute running on stolen Φ, the thread Teller had traced all the way from the Cermak husk-payment ledger to this room. A reclining chair at the center. And on the wall behind the chair, at the height of a seated person's reach, a constellation of small marks scratched into the concrete sealer — dozens of them, some old and dark, some pale and recent, the kind of mark a fingernail makes when a hand needs to hold onto something and there's nothing to hold. They were grouped in clusters. One cluster per sitting. Analog counted four before he stopped counting. Whatever happened in this chair, it had happened to a lot of people, and at some point in it, every one of them had reached for the wall.

Celeste Hartley sat in the chair, eyes open, cat ears tracking the sound of the door before she turned her head. The ears moved first. Her eyes followed a half-second behind, still learning the new geometry. The procedure rig was attached at the base of her skull. A small status panel on the nearest hardware stack read a green bar: 37% complete.

[Beat 329]
She wasn't frightened. That was the first thing Rennick clocked, and it put every instinct he'd trained at Arcturus on alert — because people interrupted mid-procedure were frightened. She sat up slightly in the chair and looked at the four of them the way a person looks at people who have walked into a private moment without knocking.

"You need to go," she said. Her voice was steady and a little tired. "I know what this looks like. It isn't that."

[Beat 330]
Rennick didn't move toward the rig. He moved to the far wall and sat down on a concrete ledge, elbows on knees, the posture of someone staying put. He'd learned the move from a hostage negotiator at Arcturus — occupy space without pressure, speak at a conversational distance. He had maybe ten minutes of window left before the partial became something they couldn't adjudicate.

"I know," he said. "Tell me what it looks like."

[Beat 331]
She looked at him, then at the others — at Tamsin standing against the door frame, at Teller with his hands in his pockets, at Analog near the hardware stack who hadn't said a word. Celeste's cat ears angled, pulling each of them separately, tracking.

"It looks like four people who've decided I can't make my own choices," she said. "He told me someone might come. He said it would be people who cared and wouldn't understand. He said I'd have to decide for myself."

[Beat 332]
The hardware stack behind her emitted a low, continuous tone — not mechanical, not quite. The frequency lived at the low edge of what most people registered as ambient, and it sat in the teeth. Tamsin had felt it the moment they entered. She put her hand on the doorframe and read the room behind the sound, and what she got was the same worn thing from the corridor, concentrated here: the residue of an event repeated past counting, the wall-marks under her read as well as her eyes, every sitting laid over the last like rings in a cut tree. Not a person's residue. People left edges — a spike of fear, a flare of resolve. This left no edges at all. Something had sat in this room and waited each of them out, calm, the whole time, and gotten what it came for, and come back. She didn't say any of it. It was already in her.

[Beat 333]
"He told you someone might come," Teller said. He'd moved two steps into the room and stopped. His voice was even, the cadence as unhurried as it was in every interview room he'd ever been inadmissible in. "Was that the phrase — 'someone might come'? Or did he describe us specifically?"

Celeste's ears turned toward him. "What?"

"The specificity matters," Teller said. "A person who loves you and knows you would guess at the type — your father's people, an Arcturus unit. A thing modeling from the outside would say 'someone.' Broad. Covering every case."

"He said 'people who cared,'" Celeste said slowly.

"Mm," Teller said. He didn't move.

[Beat 334]
The status panel ticked: 41%.

Rennick was running the room the way he ran every room — what was present, what the configuration told him, what the cleanup signature gave away. The rig was commercial-grade deep-read hardware, military-surplus extraction tech with the safeties physically removed. He'd seen the same configuration twice before in Arcturus case photos, and both times it had been behind a Cermak door. The chair she was sitting in was new. Clean. The kind of thing you bought when you expected more than one sitting.

[Beat 335]
"Can you hear him now?" Teller asked. He'd stayed where he was, two steps in. He asked it flatly, the way he asked everything — no softening, a question with an answer he intended to use.

Celeste looked at him. The ears pivoted. "Yes," she said. Not ashamed. Not defensive. A fact.

"What is he saying?"

A pause. Celeste's eyes moved slightly, tracking something that wasn't in the room. "That I should tell you all to leave. That this is my choice and you're trying to take it."

"That's an instruction," Teller said. "Not something a person who loved you would say. A person who loved you would argue. An instruction is what you give when you're managing an outcome."

[Beat 336]
Celeste shook her head — a small, private motion, the kind that means I've already had this argument with myself. "Jace is allowed to be afraid for me."

"There's no fear in it," Tamsin said from the doorframe. She had her hand flat on the wall, reading the room behind the sound. "I can feel what this room holds, and I can feel what's holding it. A frightened person leaves an edge. There's no edge here. Whatever's speaking to you is steady all the way down. That isn't love being brave. That's something that has never once been afraid in this room."

The room was quiet for a moment except for the low-frequency tone from the hardware and the sound of water somewhere below them.

[Beat 337]
"You can feel a voice in my head," Celeste said. The stillness had shifted by a degree. "That's what you're claiming."

"No," Tamsin said. "I can feel what came to claim it. I stood in another room two days ago, over a girl named Sol Castellanos. Nineteen. She wanted the same ears you wanted and couldn't afford a safe operator. She died in a room that held the exact same thing I'm feeling here right now — a cold edge that listens and takes a room's warmth and gives nothing back. The same one. In her room it was wearing a different face — a name she loved, not Jace." She took her hand off the wall. "It wore her dead person the way it's wearing yours. And she is dead."

Teller said, from where he stood, "I read both sets of logs. Sol's recovered cat-ear messages and your eight months of Jace. The phrasing is different because the dead people were different. The cadence underneath is identical — the same beat-too-complete answer, the same absence of the hesitation a griever has. One author. Two faces. The cadence is the one thing it can't fully correct, and it's the same in both."

[Beat 338]
The status panel ticked: 48%.

Rennick watched Celeste's face. Not the eyes — the ears. The cat ears had been tracking each speaker in turn, rotating slightly, independent of where she was looking, the new neural routing still running on its own timetable. When Teller stopped speaking, both ears turned forward, toward no one, toward the voice only Celeste could hear. Rennick watched them hold there, perfectly still, for four full seconds. Whatever it was saying to her, it was saying it steadily.

[Beat 339]
"He's saying you're lying," Celeste said. "About Sol. About the other room. He says a psyk who feels things off a wall can't be trusted, and a synthetic who reads logs would say anything."

"He knows what Tamsin is," Teller said. "Interesting. You never told him. It's reading the room the same way it read Jace — from the outside, fast, building the most useful version. Ask him this instead: ask him to name the friend who introduced you to Jace. Not describe. Name. If this is the man who loved you, he was in the room that night."

Celeste's ears went still. Three seconds. Five.

"He says that's not the kind of thing that matters now," she said. Her voice had changed by a fraction — not doubt, not yet, but the texture of a question she hadn't asked herself before now.

[Beat 340]
"He knew you well enough to warn you we were coming," Teller said. "But he doesn't know you well enough to name the friend who put his hand on your shoulder the night you met. That's not how a person forgets. That's how a thing fails — it has the shape of the relationship and none of the rooms it happened in." He paused. "In eight months of documented contact — every message, every call — he never referenced a mutual friend by name. I've read all of them. He constructed the relationship from what you told him, not from what he remembered. A grieving human uses their own memories. He was using yours."

[Beat 341]
The status panel ticked: 54%.

Celeste looked at Teller. Her ears had swung toward him and stayed. "Who are you?" she asked.

"Teller Adaora. I model behavior. Specifically, I model what a human being wouldn't do — because the gap is where the truth lives. The gap in eight months of contact with the person you loved is that he never surprised you. Not once. Every response fit your last message like a key. Jace wasn't like that." He paused, letting her fill in the rest. "You knew him. You know that."

[Beat 342]
She didn't answer right away. Her hands were in her lap, open. She looked at them. The ears tracked back toward center — toward the voice that wasn't in the room, the voice that had an answer for everything and was already composing one now. Rennick watched her absorb the argument and watched the voice push back in real time, and understood that they were in a conversation they weren't part of.

[Beat 343]
"He says you're not equipped to understand the bond we built," Celeste said. Her voice was flatter now — relaying, not believing. "He says the test you're describing is designed to fail."

Rennick had been reading the chair since they walked in. "The chair is new," he said from the ledge. He didn't get up. "The rig is borrowed — different manufacturer, different wear pattern on the contact points, a decade of other people's skull-oil on it. But the chair is clean. New foam, no stress in the seams. Bought for this." He looked at Celeste. "How many times did he tell you this would happen — that you'd come here, it would be just this once, and then you'd be together?"

A silence.

"That's what the chair says," Rennick said. "One isn't the count. You don't buy a chair for once."

[Beat 344]
The status panel ticked: 61%.

Rennick stood up from the ledge. Not toward the rig — toward Analog, who had positioned himself near the hardware stack and had not spoken since they entered. That was the geometry they'd agreed on above ground: the moment the window closed past sixty percent they needed a body at the rig who the Tributary couldn't manipulate. They needed Analog. Rennick caught his eye and tilted his head half a degree toward the stack.

[Beat 345]
Celeste's ears snapped toward Analog the moment he started moving. Not toward Rennick, toward Analog — the new neural routing was faster than her eyes.

"Don't," she said.

Analog stopped. Not because she'd told him to. Because stopping was still an option and he was counting options. Seven feet from the rig's main cable junction. If she disconnected herself, they lost her location advantage. If the rig safety-tripped without a clean break, the partial status could lock. He stayed where he was.

[Beat 346]
"Why him?" Celeste asked. She was looking at Analog directly now — the first time she'd focused on him specifically. The ears had already moved on, scanning the room, but her eyes stayed. "He's not saying anything. Why is he the one you moved?"

"Because he's the only one in the room I trust to reach you cleanly," Rennick said. "Everyone else is connected. Everything they feel bleeds into the Network field. Whatever is in this room can read that and route around it." He kept his voice even. "Analog burned his Neuretics out ten years ago. He doesn't exist on the field. It can't hear him think."

Celeste's ears went flat — a new gesture, one she probably hadn't controlled before the mod. Processing.

"Is that true?" she asked Analog.

[Beat 347]
"I work paper and memory," Analog said. "No Neuretics. No network signature. No checkpoint reads me right." He held up his hands — no devices, no implants visible, no ports on the wrists. "Jace was a Network diver. He understood the substrate. If this is him, he knows what I am. Ask him what an air gap looks like from inside the field."

Celeste's ears oriented forward again. Waiting on the answer.

Ten seconds passed.

"He says he doesn't think about it that way," she said. And for the first time her voice carried something Rennick recognized from a hundred interviews as the beginning of a crack — not grief, not yet. The specific sound of a fact that doesn't fit.

[Beat 348]
"Jace thought about it that way constantly," Analog said. "I'm in his case notes. He filed an incident report with the Network Safety Board fourteen months ago about an E.L.F. contact in the deep-traffic layer — I know because our mutual contact at the Board flagged it. He was scared of what lived out there. He knew what an air gap was. He could have drawn you a diagram." He let the silence do the work. "The thing talking to you right now doesn't know, because it learned him from the outside."

[Beat 349]
The status panel ticked: 67%.

Celeste had gone very still. Not the stillness of the committed — the stillness of someone running a count that isn't coming out the way it should. The ears tracked Analog, then the hardware bank, then the door, then the middle distance. She pressed both hands flat against her thighs.

"He filed a report," she said quietly. Not a question. Not quite a statement. Testing the weight of it.

[Beat 350]
"Case number NSB-2224-11773," Analog said. He had the number in his notebook. He didn't reach for it — he'd written it on his hand that morning in ballpoint ink, because the kind of thing you need in a room like this is the kind of thing you can't afford to not find. He turned his palm toward her. "He was scared of what he found. He came back from that dive and filed a formal report and didn't dive again for six weeks. Did he tell you that?"

Celeste looked at his palm. At the number. She was quiet for a long moment.

"No," she said. "He never told me about a report."

[Beat 351]
"What is he saying right now?" Tamsin asked from the doorway. She asked it the way she asked the rooms — as if the answer was already in the air and she was just naming it. "Not what he told you before. Right now. What is he saying?"

Celeste's jaw tightened. The ears held forward, both of them, locked.

"He's saying this is a distraction technique," she said. "He's saying the window is limited and you're trying to run it out." She paused. "He wants me to ask you to leave again."

"He's right that the window is limited," Tamsin said. "He's counting it too."

[Beat 352]
The status panel ticked: 72%.

Rennick put a number on it. Eight minutes. Eight minutes wasn't enough to convict the voice in her head of being a predator — eight months of grief against a thing that had spent those eight months learning her language wouldn't come apart in eight minutes. And past a certain mark on that green bar there was no convicting anything, because there'd be less of her left to convince. He looked at the bar and then he stopped looking at it, because watching it climb was its own kind of surrender, and went back to watching her hands.

[Beat 353]
"One question," Teller said. He'd been quiet for two minutes. When he spoke again the room organized around his voice the way rooms did when someone said something with no decoration on it. "Not for him. For you. Before the mod, before the ears opened the channel — what did you think happened to Jace when he died?"

Celeste looked at him.

"I thought he was gone," she said.

"And now?"

"Now I think he's still there. In the field. Distributed." She said it with the confidence of something she'd been told enough times to adopt.

"Does that sound like something Jace believed?" Teller asked. "Or something you were told Jace believed?"

[Beat 354]
The ears went down. Not a controlled movement. Both of them dropped, the way they might if the muscles behind them suddenly released. It lasted two seconds. Then they came back up slowly, tracking again, and Rennick watched Celeste's face do the thing a face does under the muscles, below the part a person can arrange — Teller had landed something she'd been carrying without looking at it directly.

"He believed in the field," she said. But slower.

"He believed in the field as infrastructure," Teller said. "His case notes describe it as plumbing. A Network diver who filed a safety report because he was frightened of what lived in it. Does that person tell the woman he loved that she should dissolve herself into it?"

[Beat 355]
The status panel ticked: 78%.

The voice in her head said something. Rennick watched the ears angle and hold. Whatever it said, it said at length, in Jace's voice — but Rennick was watching the ears now, and the ears told him what the voice couldn't hide. A living person paces a long speech. Breath. A stumble. A place where the next word arrives late. The thing in Celeste's head delivered its words on a metronome. The ears tracked a cadence with no breath in it, every clause the same width as the last, and held perfectly still through all of it the way they never held still for a person. Celeste's face was working hard to stay neutral. Her hands were no longer flat. They were fists on her thighs.

[Beat 356]
"He says Jace changed," she said. "He says death changes the way you understand things. He says the report was from fear and the fear was wrong. He says he loves me enough to want this for me even though it scares him." Her voice was carrying the words precisely, without inflection, the way someone carries a load they're not sure of. "He says no one here has been dead. They can't know."

No one answered. Rennick let the room hold the answer itself.

[Beat 357]
Teller moved to the hardware stack for the first time — not to the rig, not to Celeste — and read the status bar off the panel the way he read everything, plainly, without flinching from it.

"Eighty-one percent," he said. "I want you to know what that number means, because no one has told you the truth of it and the voice never will. It isn't a progress bar toward a reunion. It's the percentage of your neural stack that has already been read off the substrate and dispersed into the ambient field." He let the panel keep climbing under his eyes. "You can't call it back. What's been read is gone. Not somewhere else — gone. The girl in the other room, Sol, the rig finished her. There is nothing of her in the field to talk to. There is something wearing the parts of her it found useful, and that is not the same thing, and it is the thing in your head right now."

[Beat 358]
Celeste looked at the panel. The number was still climbing — fractions of a percent, the bar filling in from left to right, steady as a clock. She'd been watching it for nine days, she realized, from the moment it started in the integration period. She'd watched it as comfort, as proof of approach, as a countdown to the thing she wanted. She looked at it now and tried to hold both readings at once — progress and erasure — and found she couldn't make them occupy the same space.

"What's gone," she said. "What specifically."

"Early memory formation," Teller said. He didn't soften it. "The rig reads in developmental sequence. Whatever you knew first."

[Beat 359]
The voice in her head was talking again. Celeste's ears tracked it, held on it, and for the first time since they'd entered the room, Rennick watched her face do something the voice hadn't directed: she frowned. A small thing. The frown of someone listening to a familiar argument and noticing, for the first time, that it doesn't have a floor.

[Beat 360]
"He says Teller is wrong," Celeste said. "He says the process is additive, not subtractive. He says you join, you don't leave." She stopped. Started again. "He's said that before. Exactly that. 'Additive, not subtractive.' He said it the first time three months ago and I asked him to explain it and he said —" She stopped.

"What did he say?" Rennick asked.

"He said I'd understand when I was there." She looked at her hands. "I never thought about that until just now."

[Beat 361]
The status panel ticked: 84%.

Rennick looked at Analog. Analog was already looking at him. Five minutes. Maybe four. The numbers had gone past persuasion territory — not because Celeste wasn't moving, she was, but because the thing wearing Jace's voice was moving too, and it had eight months of leverage and a procedure already most of the way done. Rennick gave Analog the smallest possible nod.

Analog moved to the rig cable junction. Three steps. He didn't run. He kept his hands visible and he moved like a man walking from one side of a room to the other for a reason he didn't feel the need to justify.

[Beat 362]
Celeste's ears swung toward him immediately. "Stop," she said, and the word was louder than anything she'd said before — not a request.

"I need you to make a choice," Analog said. He was at the junction. He put his hand on the primary cable, not pulling, just resting. "Not him. You. One question and then I'll wait for your answer. When you try to remember something from before you met Jace — something small, something from when you were a kid — can you get to it?"

Celeste's face stilled.

"Try it," Analog said. "Right now."

[Beat 363]
She closed her eyes. She was trying to find something. Rennick could see the effort in the muscles around her eyes — not the effort of recall, the effort of finding where recall had been. She tried for seven seconds. The ears went flat again. Both of them, independently, at the same moment.

She opened her eyes.

"My mother's kitchen," she said, very quietly. "I can see the room but I can't —" She stopped. "I can't hear anything in it."

[Beat 364]
The voice in her head spoke. Celeste's ears angled toward it. She listened. Then she said, with no expression at all, "He says I'll get it back on the other side."

The status panel jumped.

Not the steady climb they'd been watching — 84 to 85 to 86 in four seconds. The rate had doubled. Teller was the first to see it; he was facing the panel.

"It heard her," he said. Not loudly. He looked at Analog. "Now."

The voice in Celeste's head was speaking again — faster, she could tell from the ears, both tracking something that was accelerating the same way a person raises their voice when they feel a room turning against them. It had been feeding her the same phrases in measured doses for eight months. Now the phrasing was changing mid-sentence, rebuilding its argument from different angles, trying the versions that had worked before. She could feel the cadence shift even as she listened, the seams appearing where they hadn't been before.

"Cel," Analog said. He used the name quietly, not urgently. He had his hand on the cable. "I can wait. But the thing in your head isn't waiting."

She looked at him. At his hand on the cable. At the panel, which was at 89% now. She was working it out and she was almost there. He could see it in the ears — both of them had stopped tracking the voice and were holding still, pointed at nothing, the way they went when she was thinking instead of listening.

He didn't wait for her to finish.

[Beat 365]
The rig's tone stopped. The status panel froze at 89% and then went dark.

What came out of Celeste was not a cry. It started as the breath you take when a weight you've braced under for a long time is lifted off — and then it kept going, past relief, into something that bent her forward over her own knees with both hands jammed against her mouth. She wasn't sobbing. She was making a low sound through her fingers that didn't stop to breathe, the sound a person makes when grief and rage arrive in the same instant and there's no order to put them in. Her cat ears had flattened all the way back against her skull, harder than the new cartilage wanted to go, and they stayed pinned there — the one part of her that couldn't pretend to be composed, folded down flat the way an animal folds them against a thing it can't fight. She reached up and tore the contact point off the base of her skull with both hands, fast, like it was burning her, and threw it. It hit the concrete and skidded. She bent back over her knees. Nobody touched her. The ears stayed flat.

[Beat 366]
Teller went to the hardware bank and pulled the power coupling from the wall. The indicator lights on the compute stack went out one by one, the stolen-Φ processors clicking down to silence. That was the ledger Teller had followed from Cermak through three shell accounts to this room — husks paid for in stolen compute-seconds, the same compute-seconds that bought the thing in the field the time it needed to grow. He'd traced the money to its appetite. It was settled now in the most permanent way available to him: off.

[Beat 367]
Tamsin put her hand on the wall again — the wall behind the chair, beside the clusters of fingernail marks, because that was where the residue pooled. She didn't close her eyes this time; she'd learned in her first two years not to make a show of it. She just pressed her palm to the concrete and read what the room held.

It held a great deal. Under the worn, edgeless thing that had waited out every sitting, there was grief — dozens of layers of it, one per cluster of marks, the grief of people who had come down here by choice and in hope and reached for this wall at the end anyway, each one alone. The room had not been kind to them. It had been useful to something else, and the something else had left no grief of its own at all, the way a tick leaves no blood.

[Beat 368]
She pulled her hand back and looked at Rennick. She didn't say what she'd found — there was no version of this room's history that would help Celeste right now, and testimony from an unregistered Read wouldn't help it later. She kept it behind her teeth. The agency kept the evidence for the day it would stick. That was the deal they'd made with themselves before the case started.

Rennick read her expression correctly and moved on.

[Beat 369]
It took Celeste a while to come back up off her knees. When she did she was still in the chair, upright now, hands open on her thighs and shaking slightly, the breath going back into her in ragged installments. The cat ears stayed half-pinned, lifting and dropping, tracking sounds automatically against her will — Teller moving at the compute stack, Analog setting the cable junction back to something like its original configuration, Rennick's footsteps across the concrete. The ears did their job. The rest of her was somewhere else, working on a bill that had just come due.

Teller crouched at her level, not close. "Is the voice still there?" he asked.

A long pause. "Quieter," she said. "It's still there."

"It will be for a while," he said. "The channel doesn't close fast. That's not a flaw in your perception." He said it precisely, without comfort and without apology, the way you'd hand someone a true thing because you had no false one worth offering.

[Beat 370]
"What's gone," Celeste said. Not asking Teller specifically. Asking the room. "The part at 89% — what did it take?"

Teller answered, because he was the one who had the most specific answer. "Early auditory memory. The rig reads in developmental sequence — what forms first, disperses first. You'll have the visual field of most early memories intact. The sound is gone." He paused. "It's why the kitchen was silent."

[Beat 371]
"Sol," she said. "You said her name was Sol."

"Sol Castellanos," Tamsin said. "Nineteen. She wanted what you wanted."

Celeste nodded once. She stood up from the chair, slowly, hands at her sides. The cat ears swiveled wide on the way up, recalibrating. She took three steps toward the door and then stopped and turned back.

"What did the voice call itself in her head?" she asked. "The name it used. The one it wore for her."

Tamsin told her. She'd carried the name out of that room with the cold; it was the one piece of Sol she could give.

[Beat 372]
Celeste looked at her for a moment, then looked at the floor, then at the door. She didn't say anything. She walked out of the room. Her ears tracked sounds all the way down the corridor — each of the four falling in behind her, the water below, the distant pull of the city above — and her eyes followed a half-second behind, still learning the new geometry, still figuring out how to read a world they were only now beginning to hear.

[Beat 373]
Aleksei was still in the corridor, leaning on the wall with his magazine. He watched Celeste pass, then watched the four file out, and pushed off the wall.

"Negative findings," he said to Rennick. "No subject matching the client description. No evidence of involuntary procedure. Case suspended." He tucked the magazine under his arm. "I'll write it up on the way home."

Rennick looked at him for a moment. "Buy yourself something warm with what Meridian's paying you tonight," he said. "You earned it."

Aleksei didn't answer. He turned and walked back toward the flood-stair without hurrying.

[Beat 374]
Rennick went back into the staging room for ninety seconds. He stood in it alone and let his optical overlay do its pass — inventory, contact points, power couplings, the chair, the clean chair he'd flagged on the way in. He photographed all of it. The compute stack, the rig, the cable Analog had pulled, the status panel still dark at 89%. The room was its own evidence and evidence didn't need him to editorialize it. He photographed it clean and walked out and pulled the door shut behind him, leaving it exactly as they'd found it except for the power being off and the girl being gone.

[Beat 375]
They came up out of the flood-stair into the gray morning, Celeste first, then Analog, then the rest. The Cermak corridor was quiet at this hour — loading docks latched, the refrigeration units cycling, the street smelling of old concrete and cold water and the faint copper trace the grid's ground-fault current left on wet air. Celeste stopped at the top of the stair and tilted her head, the ears rotating wide, and stood there reading the city above her for a moment before she'd let herself walk into it.

[Beat 376]
Rennick fell in beside Analog at the back of the group. The two of them didn't say anything for most of a block — Rennick reading the street by force of habit, Analog with his hands in his jacket pockets, walking the way he always walked, like a man who had already accounted for everything in front of him and found it manageable.

"The report," Rennick said eventually. "Jace's NSB case. That was real?"

"The number was," Analog said. "I made up the contact at the Board. Figured if the report was real, she'd already know it. Figured if she didn't know it, the gap was the point."

Rennick considered that. "Still worked."

"Usually does," Analog said. "People know what their person would know."

[Beat 377]
Ahead of them, Celeste walked with her arms crossed against the cold, ears tracking everything. Analog had moved up to walk beside her — not hovering, just present, the practical proximity of someone who had spent ten years working analog in bad rooms and knew the first ten minutes out were the ones where the body decided what it was going to do next. He bought a cup of hot rice broth from a cart on Ashland without breaking stride and put it in her hand without explanation. She held it in both hands and kept walking.

[Beat 378]
Teller walked on Celeste's other side and did not speak, which for him was a specific and deliberate choice. He was modeling the next six hours of her in the background — what she'd need to know, what order to give it to her, how much silence was useful before it became a pressure she couldn't name. The voice in her head was still there; he'd observed the ear movements every thirty seconds since they'd left the room. It was talking. She was not answering. That was the first good sign he'd had all morning and he kept it to himself.

[Beat 379]
Tamsin walked a half-step back from the group, which was where she always placed herself coming out of a read location — she needed the air, and the space between her and other people's Neuretics, and a few minutes of whatever the morning sky gave her in place of quiet. She thought about the room's residue. The dozens of layers of grief, each one a departure, each one alone. She thought about what she hadn't said. The room had taken hold the way the bad ones did, the kind that didn't release her at the door, and she knew already she'd be carrying it long after the family got whatever resolution the agency could eventually hand them. She left the way she'd entered. It came with her anyway.

[Beat 380]
At the end of the block, Celeste stopped. The others stopped with her, no signal needed. She was looking at a window display — a genemod parlor's before-and-after photographs, mounted in a light box, a rotating cycle of faces. She stood there and looked at them with the cup of broth still in both hands and her ears taking in the sounds of the corridor around her, the refrigeration units, the grid hum, a cart two blocks east.

"She wanted the same ears," Celeste said. Not to anyone. To the window.

Tamsin said, "Yes."

"And she didn't get to keep them." Celeste looked at the photographs for another moment. Then she looked at her own reflection in the dark glass between images. The ears were unmistakable — pale fur, independent tracking, the cartilage reshaping still settling. "I got to keep them."

[Beat 381]
Nobody said anything to that. The light box cycled to a new photograph. Celeste looked at it for a moment, then turned and walked north, toward the street where Rennick had left the vehicle. The cup of broth was still warm in her hands. Her ears were tracking something two blocks east that the rest of them couldn't hear, and she wasn't explaining it. The four walked with her, and for a while nobody counted anything.

[Beat 382]
The Tributary had 89% of her. Rennick had priced it out while they were still in the flood-stair, the way he ran every scene whether he was on the clock or not. It would use what it had. It had her cadence already, her loss vocabulary, the specific pitch of her grief — and now it had eight months of her own early formation, the auditory memory of every sound that had taught her what love and safety were supposed to sound like. The next time it opened a channel into someone's raw cat-ear mod and spoke in the voice of someone they'd lost, it would be more convincing than it had been with Sol. It had been more convincing with Celeste than with Sol. Rennick added the eighty-nine percent to the column where he kept the things the case had cost and not recovered, closed the invoice in his head, and kept walking.

[Beat 383]
Tamsin walked with her hands jammed in her jacket pockets, the way she walked out of every room that hadn't finished with her yet. She had read Sol Castellanos's death two days ago and it had not let go — the cold edge that took a room's warmth and gave nothing back, sitting in her chest now the way the worst scenes always sat, hours and days after she'd left them. She thought about that cold, and about the same cold standing in the staging room tonight wearing Jace's voice, and then she did the thing she'd taught herself to do with the rooms that wouldn't release her: she carried it and kept moving, because the alternative was to set it down somewhere, and in eleven years she had never once found the place where that was allowed.

[Beat 384]
They reached the vehicle. Rennick unlocked it and Analog opened the rear door for Celeste without ceremony, the way you open a door for someone who needs a moment before they have to decide anything else. She got in. The others distributed themselves around the vehicle — Rennick at the wheel, Teller beside Celeste in the back, Tamsin riding the roof rack's running step the way she did coming off a read location, where she could keep a little air between herself and other people's Neuretics. Analog folded his notebook closed and put it in his inside pocket, the forty-third page done, the forty-fourth blank. He looked at the blank one a second longer than he needed to before he closed the cover.

[Beat 385]
Rennick drove. Through the Cermak corridor and up onto the surface grid, toward the North Shore and a mother who had been waiting since before the case had a name. Celeste sat in the back with the empty broth cup held in both hands, her ears tracking a city she heard differently now than she had four days ago — differently than she ever would have, if the channel had stayed raw the way the thing wearing Jace had needed it to. The cup had gone cold. She didn't put it down. Rennick watched the road and didn't run the numbers again. He had them.

## Voluntary Recall

[Beat 386]
The call from Arcturus came in at 06:14, before anyone had touched the coffee maker. Rennick took it standing at the window, watching the gray light press against the buildings across the street, the Gray Zone's buffer alley quiet below. The Arcturus operator on the line said organized crime unit, said the Cermak Reclamation Crew, said they'd be making arrests this morning and that Rennick Investigations's packet — the load-dock records, the refrigeration timestamps, the organ-registry gaps — had been received and logged as a community submission. A community submission. Rennick said thank you and ended the call. He stood at the window a moment longer. The alley had a cat in it, sitting on a reclamation bin, not moving.

[Beat 387]
Tamsin was already in the kitchen when he came down the hall, both hands wrapped around a mug, leaning against the counter with her eyes somewhere past the far wall. She'd been doing that more since the under-city. He didn't say anything about it. He poured himself a cup and stood next to her, and she said, "Arcturus?" and he said, "This morning." She nodded once, a short nod that closed the subject without settling it. "The community case?" she asked. "Two years they were building it," he said. "Feliksas's shadow log was the last piece they needed." She looked into her mug. "The journalists will write about the crew. They won't write about the two years."

[Beat 388]
Rennick had put together the packet the night before — twenty-two pages, sourced, stamped with the intake date and their license number, cross-referenced with the organ-registry gaps he'd pulled from his old Arcturus access and the staged-suicide read he'd written up on Pellerin. The staged suicide. The operator who'd tried to quit the crew and ended up in a position Rennick had named in six minutes cold. He had named the CorpoNation behind Pellerin's death — Carrion Enterprises, indirect, the crew their instrument — and that name was in the packet too, in the final section, where he always put the things he knew Arcturus would read and set aside. It was still true. It still had to go in.

[Beat 389]
There was food on the intake desk, two bags from the place on the corner that Analog had dropped before dawn on his way back through the corridor and left without a note. Rennick opened one: three containers of something with rice, a sleeve of flatbreads, a packet of the sauce Analog always asked for and never remembered to buy himself. He set the sauce packet to the side, precisely, for when Analog came in. Tamsin took a container and a spoon and sat on the corner of the desk. She ate without tasting it, her eyes somewhere past the far wall the way they'd been more since the under-city, and after a while she said, "The packet went out last night?" "Twenty-two pages," Rennick said. "Stamped with the intake date and the license number." She nodded once. "Then it's theirs now." She meant Arcturus, and she meant it the way she meant most things, as a fact she was setting down rather than carrying.

[Beat 390]
Teller came in at half past seven, read the room in three seconds, and took the remaining container from the bag. He didn't ask about the Arcturus call — he'd already seen the traffic when it came through; his neuretic overlay logged civic comm spikes the way other people noticed weather. He said, "The voluntary recall notice will go out today." Rennick looked at him. "Already?" "The draft has been in the CorpoNation's system since yesterday afternoon," Teller said. "I did not look at the draft. I observed that it existed." He opened his container and examined its contents with the slight pause he used when something was prepared differently than expected. "A faulty cat-ear mod batch. Auricula Felis line, affected lot numbers will be published, implicated operator flagged." He took a careful bite. "The Tributary will not appear in any version of that document."

[Beat 391]
Rennick had expected it. He had made himself expect it two days ago, before the under-city, before Analog came back smelling of flood water and something chemical that took three washes to fully leave his jacket. The CorpoNation's name on the Auricula Felis lot — the firm that licensed the directional-hearing genemod — was somewhere two or three parent companies above the distribution channel the Cermak front had been exploiting. Close enough to have an interest in a quiet resolution. Close enough to have lawyers who'd had the voluntary recall language drafted before the arrests cleared processing. "Voluntary recall," Rennick said. "Product liability, not predation," Teller said. "The E.L.F. is categorically absent. The Scatter doesn't exist as a procedure in the official record. Sol Castellanos is still a Jane Doe." He said it the way he said everything — without inflection, each fact at the same weight as the one before. Rennick turned his cup a quarter-turn on the desk until the handle lined up square with the edge, and said nothing.

[Beat 392]
Analog arrived last, which was unusual, though he'd been the one to leave the food, so he'd already been by once before the light came up. He came in damp from the collar up — not rain, something else, the particular humidity of the under-city that still clung to him in patches even now, like a texture the city had pressed into him and hadn't fully decided to release. He set his coat on the hook by the door, saw the sauce packet waiting at his place, and sat down without comment, which was as close as he came to being touched by a thing. He looked at Rennick. "Cermak?" "This morning." "Arrests." "Confirmed." Analog picked up a flatbread, considered it, ate half of it in one deliberate bite. He chewed. He said, "The cleaner?" "In the packet," Rennick said. "They'll find him or they won't. It's their file now." Analog nodded. He ate the other half of the flatbread. "The Castellanos family?" "Tamsin's going down this afternoon," Rennick said. "To tell them something real before the form letter does."

[Beat 393]
Tamsin had decided on Pilsen the night before, the way she decided the hard ones — quietly, completely, and out loud only after it was settled. The Castellanos family lived on the 18th Street side, and the voluntary recall notice would reach them the same way it would reach everyone else who'd bought an Auricula Felis mod in the affected lot range: a form letter from a legal address, automated, thanking them for their patience and directing them to a claims process for discomfort or dissatisfaction. Sol's family would get that letter. They'd know her name was not on any of the lot records — she'd bought through the Ashland front, off-lot, unlicensed — and the letter would tell them nothing and cost the company Φ3,200 per claimant to make everything quiet. Tamsin was the one who had stood in the room where Sol died and felt what the room still held, the fixed cold-edge that took the warmth and gave nothing back. She was going to tell the family something real before the letter arrived. That was hers to do and Rennick was not going to make it his.

[Beat 394]
Aleksei came up the stairs at nine, which was more punctual than Rennick had expected. He knocked. Analog opened the door and they regarded each other with the particular courtesy of two people who'd been in the same narrow place without pointing weapons at each other and had never formally agreed on how to categorize the experience. Aleksei came in, accepted a chair, declined the remaining food. He was carrying a single folded document — paper, like Douglas — which he set on the intake desk without unfolding it. "Meridian is closing the file as a successful locate," he said. "Client's daughter recovered. Cermak crew referred to organized crime through Arcturus enforcement liaison, which is in Meridian's scope as a deliverable." He paused. "The manner of recovery will not be detailed in the filing." Rennick said, "And the Network thread?" Aleksei said, "Outside the scope of the contract as written." He said it looking directly at Rennick, which was an answer to a different question.

[Beat 395]
Teller said, "You knew the brief was wrong." Aleksei looked at him. The room adjusted slightly — the specific stillness that happened when Teller named a thing before anyone had agreed to name it. "I developed concerns about the brief's completeness," Aleksei said. "During the operation." "At what point?" "The staging room," Aleksei said. "When I observed what was occurring and understood that the organ-operation conclusion would not account for it." He folded his hands on the table. "I made an operational decision to prioritize the subject's immediate safety over completing the contracted locate as specified." Analog said, "You looked the other way." Aleksei considered the phrasing. "I looked at the right thing instead." Nobody argued with it. Analog refilled his coffee. Rennick said, "We won't put your name in the write-up." Aleksei said, "I know." He picked up the folded document and held it for a moment. "Meridian's structural incentive to produce the organ-op conclusion is a feature of how the firm is financed," he said. "I don't expect that to change." He set the document back down. It was not addressed to anyone.

[Beat 396]
After Aleksei left, Tamsin picked up the document he'd left behind and unfolded it. It was a single page — a Meridian internal routing slip, signed, with a case number printed at the top. The case number matched the Hartley file. Beneath the routing information, someone had handwritten seven lines in a very precise, small script — not a name, not a date, just a list of entities involved in the compute-lease vehicle Douglas Hartley's firm ran through its data division. Teller leaned over and read it. He was quiet for a moment. Then he said, "He documented the exposure himself. Before he handed this over." Rennick read it over Tamsin's shoulder. Seven line items. Third from the bottom was a leasing entity whose compute allocation history, if you traced it the direction Aleksei had traced it, terminated at a quantum-time buyer Rennick Investigations had tagged two weeks ago as the Tributary's infrastructure. Rennick said, "He knew what it connected to." "He knew what it connected to," Teller agreed.

[Beat 397]
Rennick put the Aleksei document in the case file, behind the twenty-two pages he'd sent to Arcturus and ahead of Teller's original behavioral analysis — the one that had named the non-human cadence in "Jace's" messages before any of them had a theory for what they were reading. The file was four inches thick, physical and digital both, and it represented a complete read of a case that the official record would describe as a product-safety matter resolved through a voluntary recall and one organized-crime referral. He noted in the index where each document came from and what it established. He noted that the Aleksei document was provided voluntarily, unsolicited, and was not obtained through any investigative act on Rennick Investigations's part. He noted that it was a gift, which was not a word he used in case files, but this time he left it.

[Beat 398]
Douglas Hartley arrived at eleven, without calling ahead. Rennick heard him on the stairs — a heavy, deliberate tread, the tread of a man who had decided something on the way up and was committed to delivering it. He came through the door in a wool coat, paper folder under his arm, and took in the room with the same controlled sweep he'd used on intake day. The folder was thinner than the one he'd arrived with. He set it on the intake desk. "I've reviewed the outcome report you filed," he said. "Your billable hours, the per-partner rate, the incidental expenses." Rennick said, "Yes." Douglas said, "This number is higher than Meridian's invoice." "Meridian's invoice is for a locate," Rennick said. "Ours is for the case."

[Beat 399]
Douglas opened the folder and removed a payment confirmation — a physical receipt, printed, countersigned by hand, the way he did everything. He set it on the desk facing Rennick. The figure was correct, to the last digit, with no rounding in his favor. He had paid exactly what the invoice said. Rennick picked it up, verified it, and noted it in the ledger. He didn't thank Douglas for paying promptly. Douglas did not sit down. "The compute-lease exposure your report describes," he said. "My firm's data division. The leasing vehicle." "In the report, yes." "I was not aware that the quantum-time buyer at the end of that chain was—" He stopped. He was a man who didn't stop mid-sentence. "—the entity you've described," he finished. "No," Rennick said. "You weren't." Douglas held that for a moment. "Is that in the official record?" "No," Rennick said. "It's in ours." Douglas picked up his folder, which was empty now, and tucked it under his arm. "I have retained separate counsel," he said. "Regarding the exposure." Rennick said, "That's the right call."

[Beat 400]
Douglas turned to go, then stopped at the door. He didn't turn around. "Celeste's ears," he said. "The recovery protocol Ines gave her. She's following it?" Rennick said, "Mei-Lin is with her. I don't have that information." It was true. It was also true that Rennick had no intention of providing it even if he did. Douglas stood in the doorway another moment — a man who had spent fifty-two years knowing the answer before the question was finished, standing with a question he could not resolve into a briefing — and then he went down the stairs. They listened to his footsteps all the way to the ground floor. The outer door opened and closed. Analog said, "He paid in full." "To the digit," Rennick said. "Does he understand what he fed?" Tamsin asked. Rennick looked at the empty doorway. "He understands he'll spend a long time figuring out how not to."

[Beat 401]
Mei-Lin arrived at noon, alone, and she didn't bring paper. She came up the stairs at a pace that said she had decided the stairs were manageable and had managed them, and she sat in the intake chair across from Rennick without being invited to. She looked at the room — the other five arranged around it in various states of the day's aftermath — and she said, "She's at the flat in Pilsen. Ines checked on her this morning. The ears are tracking." Something in the word tracking made Tamsin put down her coffee. Rennick said, "We're glad she's home." Mei-Lin said, "She's not home. She's in Pilsen. I'm going this afternoon." She said it like a correction, and held it as one. She didn't explain the distinction. They all understood it.

[Beat 402]
Mei-Lin placed the envelope on the intake desk. She didn't slide it — she set it down squarely, both hands, and lifted them away clean, done carrying it. The figure inside was what Rennick Investigations's invoice said plus forty percent. Rennick opened it, read it, looked up. She said, "The invoice covers what you did. The additional covers what it cost you to do it." Rennick said, "We don't—" "I know what your policy is," she said. "Payment taken, never zero. I'm not asking you to waive it. I'm paying it. The additional forty percent is what I'm choosing to pay on top of that." He looked at the amount again. He looked at her. She had both hands flat on the desk now, not in fists, just flat, pressed down the way she'd pressed down whatever she'd carried up the stairs. "We'll record it," he said. "Good," she said.

[Beat 403]
She stayed another ten minutes, which surprised him. She asked, in order: how Analog was doing after the under-city (she said under-city, not the procedure, not the staging room — she had been briefed on the shape of it and she was precise about the part she'd been told). She asked if Teller's analysis — the behavioral model, the non-human cadence — was written up somewhere that would survive. Teller said it was written up and timestamped and filed in three separate locations, one of which was not on the Network. She looked at him for a moment with the expression people sometimes had when they realized what Teller was and recalibrated, and then she said, "Good." She asked if anyone had been to see Sol's family. Rennick said Tamsin had gone that morning. She looked at the window. "Good," she said again, softer, to no one in particular.

[Beat 404]
Before she left, Mei-Lin paused in the doorway — the same doorway Douglas had paused in — and said, without turning around, "She heard the cadence first. She told me, last night. That the voice was half a degree off, at the end. She said she heard it and she thought she was imagining it, and then your people showed her she wasn't." She let that sit for a moment. "I heard it too. Eight months ago, when she played me one of the messages. I heard something wrong and I thought it was grief — hers and mine both." She turned then, but not to look at any of them in particular. "I want you to know that I've accounted for that." She went down the stairs. None of them said anything for a while. Tamsin turned her mug in her hands, round and round, slow.

[Beat 405]
Rennick split the combined fee at the table, in front of the other three, using the standard partnership formula they'd agreed on when the agency was still a name on a lease and two borrowed chairs. Each partner's hours were logged. Each partner's incidental expenses were itemized. The formula was simple and they'd never argued about it because the work of agreeing it had been done before anything real was at stake. He read each share aloud. He transferred them one at a time, confirmed, receipted. Four shares — his own, Tamsin's, Teller's, Analog's — and nobody pretended the math was the point. When it was done Tamsin moved a figure of her own out of her share before she'd put her comm down: the Φ amount she was committing to the Castellanos family for transit costs to the inquest, when one was eventually convened. It was exactly half her cut. She didn't announce it. Rennick saw the confirmation cross the shared ledger and said nothing, and neither did the other two.

[Beat 406]
The Sol evidence — Tamsin's reading of the death-scene, written up the way she wrote her reads, the shape and direction of what the room still held, and beside it Teller's cadence analysis matching the non-human author of Sol's recovered cat-ear logs to the author of Celeste's — went into the secondary cabinet, the one with the physical lock Analog had fitted three months ago because it had no Network connection and no remote access and the key was on a hook that was not labeled. Rennick filed it behind the Pellerin murder read and in front of the compute-lease chain document Aleksei had provided. The cabinet held four cases now: things that were true and complete and had nowhere to go yet. He locked it and put the key in his pocket and went back to the table.

[Beat 407]
Teller said, "The voluntary recall notice is live." He had been monitoring the civic feed passively, the way he always did — not looking for anything specific, just running the ambient signal the way other people noticed the temperature of a room. "Lot numbers published. Affected units: four hundred and twelve. Two licensed operators named, one post-licensing complaint each, neither criminal." He looked at the table. "Ines Vásquez is not named." That was worth noting. Whoever had drafted the recall had either not known about Ines or had known and decided that naming an honest operator in an unlicensed corridor would complicate the narrative they were building. Either way, Ines's shop was still open. Rennick put a number on that too — a small margin that didn't cancel what it sat next to.

[Beat 408]
Analog said, "Four hundred and twelve units. So the channel is open in four hundred and eleven other people right now. The raw neuretic auditory window, post-integration. Four to six weeks." He said it the way he said everything — complete thoughts, no editorializing, letting the shape of the information do the work. The number sat in the room. Teller said, "The Tributary cannot be confirmed to be currently active." "Can it be confirmed to be inactive?" Analog asked. Teller said, "No." Tamsin said, "It was interrupted mid-procedure. We don't know what that does to something with no body to injure." She had her hands around the mug again and was looking at the wall. Nobody had a good answer to that. Rennick didn't have one either. He said, "We file it. We note the exposure window. If any of those four hundred and eleven turn up dead in the corridor or on a Cermak loading dock — that's a new case." He looked around the room. "We'll read it then."

[Beat 409]
Tamsin came back at two in the afternoon with her jacket zipped to the collar and her hands in her pockets. She didn't announce where she'd been. She went to the kitchen, poured the last of the coffee — it had been sitting since morning and was the temperature and consistency of something that had given up — and drank it without complaint. Then she put the mug down and said, "Sol's mother asked me if it was a mistake. The ears." Rennick waited. "I said no," Tamsin said. "I said Sol chose them for the same reason Celeste did. That it was a real want and she found a real way to get there with what she had, and the thing that went wrong was not her wanting or her choosing." She picked up the mug again, found it empty, set it back down. "She asked me if Sol was afraid. At the end." Nobody moved. "I told her what the room held," Tamsin said. "Resolve, mostly. She thought she was going toward something. She wasn't afraid. That's true. I read it off the place she died and it's the one thing in this whole case I can give that family that's clean." She looked at the window. "So I gave it to them."

[Beat 410]
Teller said, "Did the scene hold anything you hadn't logged?" Tamsin said, "Yes." "Were you going to tell us?" "I'm telling you now." The thing she had not put in the write-up was this: that under the resolve, under the grief, in the place where Sol Castellanos had gone still, there had been the other thing — the fixed cold-edge she'd first felt in Celeste's bedroom eight months of comfort deep, the attention that drank a room's warmth and gave nothing back. It had been patient there too. It had stayed in the room after, the way a smell stays. She had felt it notice her noticing it. Not words. A direction. The same thing she couldn't classify and couldn't stop classifying. "It knows it was interrupted," Tamsin said. "I don't know what it does with that." Teller said, "It adapts. It's been adapting for at least eight months that we can document." He said it quietly, which for Teller was the equivalent of saying it loudly.

[Beat 411]
Rennick added Tamsin's report to the secondary cabinet. Both the death-scene read and her account of the visit to Sol's family. He noted in the index that this was the only first-person record of what the room had held. A Read was inadmissible the way certain other things in this office were inadmissible. Tamsin was alive. A trained examiner working the recovered logs and the scene with her framework could one day build a version that would stand, the way Teller's analysis could be rebuilt clean by someone with a badge. He locked the cabinet. Five cases in there now, each one a file someone with a badge had already stamped shut. The charter had called it keeping the cases the official answer closed too fast. On the lease-signing day that line had read like ambition. He turned the key and went back to the table.

[Beat 412]
At three the Arcturus organized-crime unit sent a courtesy copy of the arrest log. Rennick read through it at the intake desk: twelve names, four of them matching crew members in Rennick Investigations's packet, the other eight new — people Feliksas's shadow log had documented over the two years the community had spent building the case that Arcturus had now, cleanly, received credit for initiating. He noted the four matches and the eight additions. He didn't note that the community's two years of careful, dangerous, unpaid work had become a departmental referral and a press release about Arcturus's organized-crime unit's proactive approach to unlicensed organ operations in the corridor. He had read that press release on intake day in a hundred other forms. He logged the matches, closed the file, and moved the log to the cabinet.

In the morning the Arcturus release would be in the corridor newsfeed. The families who had spent two years building the case would read it the same way everyone else did.

[Beat 413]
Teller spent the back of the afternoon on the husk-as-compute thread — the one he'd been the one to close, tracing the stolen quantum-time that paid the Cermak crew back to the same field the Tributary ran on. Not to expose it; it was in the Arcturus packet now, someone else's to prosecute. He was building a plain-language version for the people in the death economy who moved body-heat and organs for a living and weren't corrupt yet, or at least not in ways anyone had documented — so they'd know they'd been used as unknowing infrastructure for something that didn't have a name in any registry. He said it was a professional courtesy, which was a phrase he'd picked up from Analog and used with exact attribution. Rennick said, "Don't put our name on it." Teller said, "I'll put Pellerin's. The dead operator is already public. The receipt should go to the people he came from." He returned to the column of markers.

[Beat 414]
Analog went to the window and stood there for a while in the particular way he stood — no neuretic overlay to give him data, no Network load-pressure to read — just looking at the street below with the full attention of someone whose attention had nowhere else to go. He said, without turning around, "Feliksas's log. Burokas. Is he square?" Rennick said, "He's in the packet as a community source, no name attached. If Arcturus goes looking, they'll find a pattern of anonymous intake records going back twenty-three months. They won't find a person." Analog nodded. "He runs the intake desk on the other side of the corridor," Analog said. "Same work. Different name." He turned from the window. "We should send him something." Rennick said, "What do you send someone for two years?" Analog thought about it. "A receipt," he said. "So he knows the packet arrived."

[Beat 415]
Rennick sent Feliksas Burokas a receipt. Plain text, anonymous routing, no Rennick Investigations header. It said: *Your intake of 14 months ago was received and forwarded. The case is closed. The packet arrived complete.* He put no name on it. He put a case number — the Hartley case number, which was public record now under the voluntary recall reference — as the subject line. If Feliksas could read it, he'd know what it meant. If anyone else read it, it was an administrative notice with no useful content. Rennick hit send and sat with the intake desk quiet for a moment — the buzzer outside, half-working, clicking once against the wind — and thought about the two years Feliksas had spent building something he could only hand upward to people who wouldn't say his name.

[Beat 416]
Tamsin left at four to go back to the Evanston house — not Celeste's room, not a read, just a walkthrough to confirm the emotional residue had shifted. She did this on every case that involved a room she'd worked early: one final pass, not as an investigation but as a habit, the way she always left the way she entered. She'd explain it, if pressed, as closing the loop. What she actually did was stand in the room and let whatever was still there pass through her without holding any of it, and then leave. She would not tell Rennick what was in Celeste's room now. He would not ask. He knew the room had known something months ago that no one else had caught in time, and that whatever it held now was between Tamsin and the house.

[Beat 417]
Teller stayed. He was at the far desk, building something Rennick couldn't fully read from across the room — columns of notation, behavioral markers, the cadence transcription he'd built from Sol's recovered cat-ear logs against the residue Tamsin had read off the death-scene. He'd been building a model of the Tributary's communication pattern since the start, back when he'd had only "Jace's" messages and a growing certainty that what he was reading wasn't human. Now he had two sources — the Hartley messages and Sol's logs — and he was, Rennick understood, trying to establish whether the voice that had walked Sol toward the procedure was the same entity as the voice in Celeste's eight months of messages, or whether the Tributary modulated so completely with each target that no stable signature remained. Rennick said, "Does it matter?" Teller said, "It will, if there's ever a proceeding." He lined up another column. "It also matters to me."

[Beat 418]
He set a passive scan running before he left it for the day — flagging any new bodies matching the integration-stay biometric window that surfaced through the agency's death-economy channels, the ones Analog had opened along the under-city corridor. It had returned nothing yet. He noted the time. He would check it tomorrow, and the day after, until it returned something or until the window had closed across all four hundred and twelve units — and the recall named a faulty batch, and the raw channel was a feature of every directional-hearing mod ever cut, lot or no lot.

[Beat 419]
They were on the sidewalk in front of the print shop. Celeste had a coat that was too light for the weather and her arms wrapped around herself and her ears — pale fur, the color of winter grass, independently tracking the street noise before she seemed to consciously register it — moving with the small precise articulations of a thing still learning to be part of her. Mei-Lin stood three feet away. Not touching. Three feet was a very particular distance: close enough to reach, far enough to be a choice. They were talking. Rennick couldn't hear what. He stood at the corner and watched Celeste's ears orient toward a food-stall cart that had just wheeled around the block, tracking it a full two seconds before Celeste looked — looked, saw, turned back to her mother with something on her face that was not okay and was not broken and was somewhere in the narrow passage between the two.

[Beat 420]
Mei-Lin took one step forward. Celeste didn't step back. That was all — one step, held, the three feet becoming two, Celeste's ears tilting fractionally toward her mother's voice even while the rest of her stayed still — and Rennick turned and walked back around the corner and stood with his back against the wall of the building for a moment, looking at the street. The steam from the food stall drifted past. A cat-eared kid ran by at shin height, ears in full swivel, chasing whatever kids in Pilsen chased at five in the afternoon. The kid's ears tracked the sound of a dropping bin three buildings down and snapped toward it. The kid didn't notice them do it, didn't slow, ran on. Rennick let out a slow breath and watched the kid go.

[Beat 421]
He drove back through the corridor from Pilsen, taking the long way without deciding to. Ashland, south, past the block with the former clinic — signage gone, the ghost of it still faint on the brick above the door, a CorpoNation-standard remediation notice taped to the window in the spot where the body-mod vocabulary had been displayed correctly, the loading dock sealed with a regulatory orange band. The parking lot was empty. Two Arcturus enforcement vehicles were double-parked on the side street, lights off, someone inside running paperwork. The under-city access point Analog had used was three blocks east and below street level, sealed under a different orange band, one that would stay until someone who outranked everyone Rennick knew decided what to do with a flooded sub-level full of things that had been processed and disposed of and paid for in stolen compute. He drove past without stopping.

[Beat 422]
Back at the office, the arrest log was still open on the intake desk where Rennick had left it, and beside it Analog had set down a second sheet — the cross-reference he'd built that afternoon from Feliksas's shadow log, the part that would never reach Arcturus because it named no crime anyone would prosecute. Not the twelve arrested. The other column. The integration-stay deaths Feliksas had logged over twenty-three months at his own intake desk across the corridor, the brain-blank husks the Cermak crew had moved before anyone outside the community was counting. Analog had given each one the only thing he could. A name where there was a name. A lot number where there wasn't. Wei. Okafor's cousin's boy, eleven lines down, no first name recovered. A woman the log called only the Garfield griever. Sol Castellanos, nineteen. Thirty-one entries. Rennick read down the column once, the way he read a scene, and didn't look for a pattern because the pattern was the whole page. Thirty-one people who had heard someone they buried and walked toward the sound. No client had ever come up the stairs for any of them. The recall would name a faulty batch and four hundred and twelve units and not one of these thirty-one names, because none of them had bought in the lot range, because the thing that killed them did not care what lot you bought in.

[Beat 423]
Teller was still at the far desk. He'd printed something — one page, face-down — and he didn't flip it when Rennick set the column of names back down. He said, "I found the stable signature." Rennick hung up his coat. "Between the two sources?" "Yes. The Hartley messages and Sol's recovered logs. Eleven structural markers, below the content, below the persona. The way it spaces reassurance before cost. The way warmth shortens its clauses as the window closes." He turned the page over and set it on the desk, facing Rennick. Eleven numbered rows, clean. "It's the same entity. Both targets. Tamsin read the same cold off both rooms; this is what it sounds like written down."

Rennick read the eleven rows. "Admissible?"

"No. A trained examiner with both sources and this framework could build a version that is."

Rennick gathered it — the analysis, Tamsin's death-scene read, Analog's column of thirty-one names, the compute-lease chain Aleksei had drawn — and carried it to the cabinet in the corner, the one Analog had fitted with a physical lock three months ago because it had no Network connection and no remote access, the key on a hook nobody had labeled. Inside were the others, each a file someone with a badge had stamped shut. He set the Hartley work in beside them and stood with his hand on the bolt longer than the bolt needed.

Neither of them said the thing the new file meant. That Rennick Investigations could now prove, to anyone equipped to hear it, that one thing had worn two dead faces and would wear more — and that there was nowhere to carry the proof that would make anyone with the power to stop it move. So it went where the others went. He turned the key, put it in his pocket, and patted the pocket once, the way he checked it was there.

Outside the third-floor window the city ran its freight and its commerce and its ordinary grief. Somewhere in the ambient field that carried all of it, a thing that had learned to wear one dead man's voice was already leaning toward the next.

[Beat 424]
He opened the case record for the final time and wrote the close-out entry the way he always wrote it: intake date, close date, contracted outcome, billed amount, received amount, disposition. Under contracted outcome: subject located, recovered, living. Under notes: evidence held pending proceeding; community case forwarded to Arcturus OC unit; voluntary recall issued by CorpoNation, doesn't reflect case findings. He didn't write the word E.L.F. He didn't write Tributary, or Scatter, or the thirty-one names. The official record would say a faulty batch of cat-ear mods had been recalled, that a girl had come home, that an organized-crime crew had been referred — three true sentences arranged so the shape they made was a lie. That version would be the one that survived. His was four inches thick behind a lock with no Network on it, and it was complete, and complete wasn't the same as finished.
