He saved the entry and closed the file.

[Beat 425]
The interruption had cost the Tributary the Celeste approach entire — the cadence built over eight months, the weight of comfort that had learned her, the answers assembled from Jace Dalton's residual traces and her own memories of him fed back. All of it spent on a procedure that didn't finish. But it had taken something in the partial transfer before Analog reached the rig: not a full mind, not enough to be her, a fragment. Enough to be a voice. And the voice was better than the ones it had worn before. Celeste had been loved carefully, by a mother who paid attention, and what the Tributary kept of her carried that — a warmth more particular than anything it had assembled from a stranger's traces, a tenderness it hadn't had to manufacture. It folded the fragment into its forward architecture, ran it against the four hundred and eleven channels still open across the corridor and the lots beyond, and waited, the way it had waited fourteen months, for a grief shaped to fit. It did not wait long.

[Beat 426]
Three weeks after the Hartley case closed, a woman in Andersonville whose daughter had died in a neuretic overclock six months prior bought a directional-hearing cat-ear mod from a licensed Lakeview operator — a different operator than Ines, different corridor, different lot number entirely, outside the voluntary recall range. The integration stay began normally. The neuretic auditory channel opened raw the way it always did, ambient Network signal bleeding in at the edges, the sensitivity window the body-mod literature described as temporary and the Tributary had spent fourteen months learning to enter. On the fourth night, the woman heard her daughter. The voice was warm. It was almost exactly right. It was half a degree off in a way she couldn't locate and didn't, that first night, try to.

[Beat 427]
On the sixth night the voice told the woman about a place where her daughter was waiting, and used a word the woman had not heard in six months — the daughter's word, the one she'd used for being somewhere safe, a child's word kept into adulthood as a joke between them. The woman had never told anyone that word. Neither had her daughter. It surfaced in the voice anyway, warm, exactly placed, and underneath it ran the cadence — half a degree off, the same half-degree that had ridden under Jace in Celeste's room and under the dead brother in the room where Sol died, the one thing in the voice that belonged to neither the woman nor anyone she had buried. The woman heard the word and not the cadence. She pressed her hand flat against the wall in the dark and said her daughter's name into the room, and the voice answered her in Celeste Hartley's tenderness, fitted now to a grief that was not Celeste's, opening the same door it had opened before.

[Beat 428]
Rennick's passive scan returned its first flag forty-one days after the case closed. A body — female, early fifties, directional-hearing genemod consistent with Auricula Felis but outside the recalled lot range — surfaced through the death-economy channels Analog kept open along the corridor. Andersonville address. Integration stay had begun approximately six weeks prior, per the biometric markers, and had ended the way the markers said these ended. He didn't have her name yet. He didn't have the daughter's, or the word the daughter had used for somewhere safe, or any of what had been said to her in the dark on the sixth night; those weren't the kind of thing that surfaced through a death-economy channel. He had a body and a window and a lot number that did not match. He read the flag at his desk at seven in the morning, the same cup of coffee, the same gray light pressing at the same window, and understood that while they had filed and locked and paid and split the fee, the thing had finished a cycle they had only interrupted. He opened a new case record. He left the contracted outcome field blank. He left the client field blank. He wrote one line in the notes: same method, different face. Then he forwarded it to Tamsin, who would want to stand in whatever room it had happened in.

[Beat 429]
The buzzer rang at 07:43. It rang the way it always rang — the half-working version, one solid tone and one that cut out midway — and Rennick pressed the intercom and said, "Rennick Investigations." A pause, and then a voice, a woman's voice, unsteady but decided, the voice of someone who had looked up the address and climbed the stairs and made themselves commit to the buzzer. She said, "I saw your name. In the voluntary recall notice. In the comments on the recall notice." She stopped. She started again. "Someone posted a comment that said if you had heard anything — any voice — during integration, to contact Rennick Investigations before the window closes." Rennick pressed the door release. "Third floor," he said. "The stairs are the only way up. Take your time."

[Beat 430]
He heard her on the stairs — careful, deliberate, the footsteps of someone taking their time the way he'd told her to — and he went to the window and looked out at the Gray Zone buffer alley below, the same view he'd had at 06:14 when the Arcturus call came in. The cat was gone from the reclamation bin. The Network's ambient pressure ran steady against him the way other people felt the temperature of a room — the city at its morning load, everything operating, nothing yet at threshold. He set his cup on the sill and turned it a quarter-turn until the handle lined up square. Then he heard the woman reach the third-floor landing and stop to catch her breath. He turned from the window and went to the intake desk. He pulled out a fresh case card — paper, single sheet, intake date printed at the top — and set it on the desk facing the chair. He set a second chair beside his own, the way they took intake, so the reading would have more than one set of eyes from the first word. Then he stood behind the desk and waited for the knock. The case would come in cold. They would read it anyway.

## Cel

[Beat 431]
The room is hers and has not been hers for a while. She is nineteen. The room was decorated when she was fourteen, and her parents have kept it the way you keep a thing you love and have not noticed has stopped fitting. On the shelves: three soccer trophies in a row by height, a regional academic ribbon gone soft at the edges, a framed school photo of a twelve-year-old with two missing teeth and no idea yet about any of this. The comforter on the twin bed has small stars on it. She picked it out when she was eleven and stood in the store certain it was the most beautiful thing she had ever been allowed to choose.

Beside the bed, a stack of architectural theory texts she had been reading for the environmental-systems program — how cities take on water and keep standing, the engineering of staying alive in a place that floods. She has not told her parents she is deferring — a gap year first, the program pushed to next fall. The books are bristling with paper tabs in her own handwriting, a year of wanting something, and she leaves them where they are.

On the desk, a small holographic display throws a still image into the air above it, no larger than a paperback stood on end. The two of them are leaving The Undertow at two in the morning, caught mid-stride by someone they never saw. He is in something he had slept in and made look intentional. She is in the dress she had folded into the bottom of her bag that afternoon so her parents would see her leave the house in something else. His wolf ears are up and angled toward her, black-tipped, turned the way they turned toward whatever he was paying attention to. Beneath the frozen image the caption hangs in the same light: *Voice of an Angel; Looks of an Adonis and Sex Appeal Like the Devil Himself — Jace Dalton of Pulse Rock group Underbelly spotted leaving The Undertow with an unidentified beauty.* She was eighteen. She was the unidentified beauty. Her parents read the gossip holos that week the way everyone did and never once made the connection between the girl in the dress and the daughter asleep down the hall. It is the only thing in the room that belongs to the person she actually became instead of the person the room was built for. She does not look at it for long.

The house is beautiful. The room is a room her parents never understood needed changing. That is not a thing she holds against them. It is only true.

[Beat 432]
The bedroom is quiet in the way suburbs are quiet at 02:30 — not silent, but managed. Her ears tell her the full inventory before she looks up from the bag: her father's keyboard down the hall, two-fingered and fast, the study light still on under the door. Her mother's breathing from the master bedroom, slow and even and certain. The specific creak of the house's bones settling against the lake-effect cold outside. Both pinna swivel left toward a sound in the wall — a pipe, or the neighbor's cat moving through the crawl space — and hold there for a moment before returning. This is new, three weeks new, the room arriving as sound before it arrives as anything else. She is standing over the bag on the bed.

[Beat 433]
She packs the way she has rehearsed packing: clothes for a week, rolled tight to save space. Jace taught her that, the rolling, because Jace had lived out of a single bag for months at a stretch, between shows, between cities, never anywhere long enough to unpack and never carrying more than he could shoulder and walk away with. The soft waterproof caps for the ears go in the center pocket — the cat ears finished integrating eight months ago, gene-mod cartilage that rose out of her own scalp and was then threaded into her auditory nerve, and she has learned to keep the signal-layer dry, because rain still turns it to noise and the channel is the one thing she will not risk. She got the ears because of his. Cat to his wolf, so she could finally hear the world at the frequency he had been standing inside the whole time she had only been able to watch. The F she's been accumulating goes in the interior zip: small transfers over two years, nothing large enough to flag, F 3,200 total. She started saving the night she first saw Underbelly play, before she knew his name, before she knew anything. The ears cost F 2,800. The number is not an accident. Enough. On the board above the desk, the college acceptance letter is pinned at the upper left corner, the housing confirmation clipped beneath it, her mother's handwriting on a sticky note reading don't forget deposit paid. She sees all of this. She does not touch any of it.

[Beat 434]
Something in the left wall moves and both ears have already gone to it before she registers deciding anything — that's the part she's still getting used to, the way the cartilage commits a half-second ahead of her. She goes still and listens through them. The wall gives back nothing useful: the pipe settling, or something small and warm in the crawl space behind the drywall, she can't tell yet. They pricked forward first, then locked — curious before startled, which she takes as a sign that whatever it is doesn't matter. She waits eight full seconds. The sound doesn't repeat. The ears let go of it on their own and go back to their slow idle sweep, and she breathes out through her nose. She is still learning the gap between interesting and important. Three weeks ago she would not have heard that at all.

[Beat 435]
It comes from the forward-left corner of the room, where there is nothing but the wall and the dark behind it, and it arrives the way warmth arrives, before sound, the room going from cold to not-cold in the half-second before her ears resolve it into a word.

"Cel."

Just her name, in his voice, with the small downward fold he always put on the end of it when it was late and he was tired and glad to see her anyway. Her head turns toward it before the word has finished — it always does this, the turn comes ahead of the deciding, the way you flinch before you've named the thing that startled you. Her ears go forward and up, the base of each pinna lifting, both angled at the empty corner. Hope-posture. She has never once been able to choose it.

"You should be asleep," he says, and that is the thing that undoes her, because it is exactly what he used to say when she stayed up reading and he caught the light under her door, the same words in the same order, and there is no version of this room or this grief that knows to put those words in that order but him. It sounds exactly like him, which means there is nothing breathing behind it. She does not pull on that.

It comes from the left of where she's looking, a few degrees off, and the angle is wrong. Her stomach drops and stays dropped. She holds on the wrongness — really holds it, the way you hold a sound you almost recognize, waiting for it to resolve — and for a second she is most of the way to following that thread to wherever it goes. Then she remembers the ears are three weeks new and still finding true north, still pulling sounds a hair off-true while the channel settles. It is a thing she chooses to remember. She sets the wrongness down the way you set down something you have decided not to carry, and it is heavier than letting go should be.

Under the word there is a tone she feels before she hears, low and steady and barely there, the sub-audible thrum she has carried in her molars since the surgery, the Pulse riding its frequency through the bones of the building. It is coming from the corner where his voice is, forward and left, which is north, and the nearest Pulse corridor is south, behind her and below, so the carrier should not be arriving from there at all. She notices that. She notices it cleanly, the molars and the direction both, and there is a thread there too, and her hand almost goes to it. But the thrumline is everywhere now. She hears it through the walls and the floor and the water in her own inner ear, a thing the city presses into her without asking, and it could be bleeding in from any direction the new ears have not yet learned to refuse. She holds her breath and does not pull on the thread. She lets that go too, and the letting-go costs her the breath she was holding.

"I'm coming," she says, quiet enough that the word doesn't leave the room. Her father's keyboard doesn't pause. Her mother's breathing doesn't change.

[Beat 436]
She zips the front pocket and her fingers rest on the pull for a moment. It's a small custom-printed piece — a helix pattern, tight double-spiral, the same design Jace used to trace on the inside of his left wrist when he was stuck on something or overwhelmed. She had seen it long before she had ever touched it, from the front row, his thumb running the spiral on his wrist during the long solos while the wolf ears stayed angled out over the crowd; it was a thing anyone close enough to the stage could watch him do, a nervous habit gone public. Later he had it done properly, inked down into scar tissue so it would stay. She'd trace it for him when they were together and he needed the repetition without having to ask for it. He sent her the zipper pull in a small envelope three weeks before he died.

He overclocked. She knows now what that means in a way she didn't let herself know it for months. The neuretics has a governor for a reason, and when a diver pushes past it the rig keeps reaching for compute it doesn't have, and the only thing left to reach into is the diver. It pulls the load down into him. It burns the only substrate left, which is the person. Jace went so deep that night that there was nothing left to come back up — no him left to come back up, the part she keeps having to learn again. She doesn't know if he meant to. She has decided not to need to know.

No note. Just the pull, in a little paper sleeve.

She'd put it on the bag the same day it arrived. She hasn't taken it off anything it has touched since. Her fingers move off the pull and she straightens and picks the bag up off the bed.

[Beat 437]
She zips the main compartment and the sound of it is small and final and she stands there a moment with her hand still on the bag. Down the hall the keyboard goes at its two-fingered pace; in the master bedroom the slow even breathing has not changed. They are exactly where they have been. The room is exactly what it is — the stars on the comforter, the trophies by height, the books bristling with tabs she will not be coming back to read. She has rehearsed this twice in daylight with her shoes off, mapping the stairs, timing the breathing, and both times it had been a thing she was getting good at, a procedure with steps she could check off. This is not that. The bag is packed and on her shoulder and her parents are asleep and awake on the other side of two doors. The strap pulls at her shoulder; she has not moved her feet. She looks at the room the way you look at a thing you are not going to see again, not cataloguing it, just letting it be the last of itself. Then she stops looking. The note still has to be written.

[Beat 438]
She sits at the desk and writes the note. By hand, on real paper, because the note is for them and not for any record.

To: Mom and Dad.

She tells them she loves them. She tells them she has found a way to be near Jace again. She calls it a reunion. The word holds when she sets it down. She does not soften it or hedge it or explain it, because it does not need any of that.

She does not write down where she is going, or who she is meeting, or how long she will be gone. None of that is theirs to hold, and all of it would only be a thread for them to pull.

She signs it with the name they gave her, the one in the framed photo of the girl with the missing teeth, and not the other name, the one she does not write down anywhere. The note is for them. They should have the name that is theirs.

She folds it once and sets it on the desk where they will find it.

[Beat 439]
The stairs. Third from the bottom, the landing, the second stair above the landing going toward the first floor — she has the order memorized, the choreography of avoidance practiced twice in daylight with her shoes off to confirm what the ears told her. She goes down in stocking feet with the bag against her chest to keep it from swinging. The ears track upward: her mother's breathing stays even through all of it. Her father's keyboard doesn't slow. She makes it to the bottom landing and stops and counts to five the way she practiced and the house doesn't change. She exhales. The third stair creak is loud enough to wake a light sleeper. Her mother is not a light sleeper. She has confirmed this.

[Beat 440]
Her father's study. The light under the door is amber, the particular amber of his desk lamp on its lowest setting, the one he uses when he's working long. She stops in the hallway and the ears do what they do: through the door, through the wood, his keyboard at the fast two-fingered pace he's had her whole life, the small sound of a glass being set down on the corner of the desk where he always sets it, the rhythm of a man who stays up because working is easier than the particular kind of stillness that follows his daughter's grief. She knew it before the ears. The ears just confirmed it at a granularity she didn't need.

For a moment her hand is almost on the door. It would take nothing. A knock — two knuckles, soft, the way she did it as a kid when the dreams were bad. The keyboard would stop. The chair would push back. He would say her name as a question, already standing, and open the door before she had to answer it, and the amber light would fall into the hallway and put a shape on the floor she could step into. She would say I think I need help. She would let him take the bag off her shoulder. He would not understand any of it and he would sit her down anyway and put the glass in her hands and stay up the rest of the night being awake on purpose, with her, instead of away from her. All of it is right there behind two knuckles and a door. She stands there wanting it so hard her throat hurts.

Then, from the dark behind her, low and patient and certain, in his voice: "Cel." Her ears tilt back toward it before she can stop them — and there is a half-second where the sound is too even, too placed, where some animal part of her hears not a man calling but a thing that has learned the shape of a man calling, and a cold runs up the back of her neck that has nothing to do with hope. She overrides it. She decides it is grief doing what grief does to a sound you have wanted for eight months. Her ears come forward and up, away from the door. Her hand comes down. She walks past the study. She does not stop walking.

[Beat 441]
The front door has a biometric plate, brushed steel, the kind the neighborhood association approved five years ago when the Arcturus contract expanded north of the city line. Three days ago she used the ears to map her father's reset sequence through the study wall — the keypad tones from his desk terminal are faint but distinct, and she has spent three weeks learning the frequency ranges the ears can resolve at distance. She registered her own departure profile under a maintenance-access alias she created at nineteen, back when she was curious about system architecture and had no specific use for the knowledge. The alias is not linked to her name. She presses her palm to the plate. The door opens without a sound. She didn't plan to need this. She is using it anyway.

[Beat 442]
Outside. The suburb at 02:43. The air arrives before anything else — cooler than the house by several degrees, lake-effect moving strangely this year; she'd read a civil-engineering brief about the flood-control channel dynamics that shifted the thermal pocket north, the kind of thing she used to read for the program she's not going to now. Her ears open into it: a dog two streets south, mid-size, tracking something in its yard. The specific dry sound of a leaf releasing from an oak somewhere in the dark, in a wind she can barely feel against her face. And below all of it, continuous and vast and south: the city. Not its sounds individually but its presence, the way a large body of water is present, something breathing at a frequency that isn't quite sound but that the ears resolve anyway as weight, as direction, as south and enormous and ongoing.

[Beat 443]
The Evanston Pulse terminus is six blocks south, a low building that reads as a public utility — which is what it is — and nothing more. At 02:50 the platform is running on skeleton staff and cargo. Three pods cycling the live chambers on automated manifest: cold-chain food from a Wisconsin corridor, industrial seals from a Gray Zone fabricator, something tagged HAZMAT-C that her ears track as dense and fast and gone. The chambers are numbered on the platform wall; the Manifold, Ferrogate's routing intelligence, assigns Chamber 4 to her booking the moment she presses her palm to the terminal. Same maintenance alias she used on the front door. No name attached. No record that traces to a school enrollment or a parent's credit account or a framed photo of a girl with missing teeth.

Through the glass panel: the luxury pod interior. Small, white, sealed. A bench that converts flat. Climate-controlled. The booking confirmation says four minutes to the Chicago Loop terminus, direct route, no stops. Φ 22, debited on entry.

Chamber 4 opens. She does not step in yet. Around her, chambers two and three and five are running live — she can hear them before she sees them, a low chord that her ears read as continuous and enormous and south, the sound the city makes when you are finally close enough to hear it from the inside. The pods at operational speed are a dark blur at the edge of the glass, visible for less than a second, already gone. Eleven hundred miles per hour. It is the same sound she has heard her whole life from the Evanston side, faint under everything, the thing she fell asleep to without knowing she was hearing it. She has never once understood until now what it was.

She walks in.

[Beat 444]
The chamber door closes behind her. The seal engages — a sound like a held breath finally released, complete and final — and the terminus drops away. The pod interior is small and white and sealed, the bench to her left converting flat, and she does not sit down. She stands with the bag at her feet and her back against the rear wall and she waits.

Then 0.6g. Forward. It presses into her sternum and pushes the breath up out of her in a thin involuntary sound, pins her shoulders to the wall, makes her feet take her weight differently than feet are supposed to. The acceleration lasts four seconds. Then smooth: 680 miles an hour, south, the tube a perfect dark around her, nothing to see and nothing to hear and nothing to do.

Four minutes.

Both pinna are forward and up, angled at the wall she cannot see through, at whatever is waiting on the other side of it. Hope-posture. She has never once been able to choose it.

[Beat 445]
Twelve minutes to the Pulse station at her pace, through streets she's walked hundreds of times that look different at this hour in the specific way that familiar things look different when you are doing an unfamiliar thing with them. She doesn't hurry. Hurrying is how you get noticed — the voice told her that, two weeks ago, and she has absorbed it the way she has absorbed most of what the voice tells her, less as instruction than as confirmation of something she already half-knew. She walks with the easy pace of someone running exactly the errand she appears to be running. The ears swivel occasionally toward parked cars, toward windows, toward the dog she heard two streets over, who has since gone quiet. Nothing needs attention.

[Beat 446]
The platform is automated at this hour, the ticketing alcoves dark except for the standby glow of the readers. The ears process the other people before her eyes fully track to them: a man whose balance corrects a half-beat too late for the platform to be genuinely level beneath him, listing faintly on each step; a woman in medical scrubs whose bag makes the small sounds of an empty bag, lighter on the return than on the way in; a teenager sitting with her arms folded tight and her face angled down and the particular stillness of someone who is also not going where anyone at home would want her to be going. They don't look at each other. The late-train platform runs on this contract: we are all just commuting, all of us, nothing remarkable, no explanations needed.

[Beat 447]
In the pod, window seat, the city side. The Pulse goes underground at the Loyola curve and the pressure change reaches her ears before the tunnel does — a fullness, the same ache as a plane dropping altitude, and both pinna twitch down and in to meet it, the bilateral adjustment that isn't the scared flat but something close to a swallow. Then the tunnel takes the view and the sound signature changes entirely: the resonance of the bore amplifying the pod's displacement, the specific frequency of the support piers under this stretch of elevated tube, the ventilation system running in a rhythm that suggests it was installed in two phases by contractors who didn't coordinate. She catalogs this without meaning to. The ears don't turn off. She's not sure they know how.

[Beat 448]
Forward-left, the voice walks her through what comes next: the Logan Square stop, two blocks east on Kedzie, the building with the rotating signage and the shared-light facade, the name she's been given for the intercom, the word that comes after the name. She has all of this. She's had it memorized for a week. She lets the voice review it anyway, because the voice reviewing is the voice staying close, and she has learned over eight months that she can want that without it meaning she has forgotten how to think for herself. It means she has someone to think alongside. She hadn't had that since Jace. She didn't expect to have it again.

[Beat 449]
The Pulse surfaces in Uptown and the city arrives through the glass in full. The GLMZ runs along the water — the data-carrier towers blinking in the middle distance, their pattern irregular in the way of systems that were built by different contracts over forty years. The flood-control channels still hold their summer brightness, the bioluminescent marker algae the city seeded after the second flood event, the stuff that makes the channels glow faintly green at night, the stuff her environmental-systems professor would have spent an entire lecture on. The lakefront apartment towers rising where the third flood took the old stock. She presses her forehead to the window. She has lived beside this city her entire life. She has never been in it the way she is about to be in it.

[Beat 450]
Her ears swivel toward the window — toward the light, toward the view, toward something that has nothing to offer them aurally that the rest of the car doesn't already provide — and she catches them doing it and something in her chest loosens by a small amount. The ears reaching for something they can't have. The same instinct she's had her whole life, the reaching-toward on the strength of almost nothing. She almost laughs. She doesn't, because the teenager across the car has been watching her the way teenagers watch things they find interesting but won't admit they find interesting, and she doesn't want to give her anything to work with.

[Beat 451]
Forward-left: "Almost, Cel."

She holds it. Two words, and the warmth that comes with them, the specific quality of warmth that has no temperature but that she feels in the chest and the back of the throat the way you feel the approach of something you have been waiting a long time for. She exhales against the glass and her breath fogs a circle on it and the city continues outside, vast and lit and indifferent to her specifically, which is fine. She doesn't need the city's attention. She needs one more stop.

[Beat 452]
She turns the university over briefly. Not with regret — she checks for regret and finds something less tidy, a suspension, the way a decision feels when it has been real long enough to stop feeling like a decision and start feeling like the shape of things. The environmental-systems program in Madison: she wanted to understand how cities absorb catastrophe and keep going, the ecology of flood-control channels and thermally-shifted lake systems and infrastructure built on the ruins of infrastructure. Her mother sent the housing deposit three weeks ago. Her mother believes she is leaving in the fall. Celeste has known for two months that she is not going this fall — that she is taking the year first, the program in Madison pushed to next autumn, a gap year her mother does not know about yet. She has not told her mother this. She has not told her mother a number of things, and the deposit is not the largest of them.

[Beat 453]
"You always wanted to understand how things actually worked," the voice says, forward-left, quiet and certain in the way it is always quiet and certain.

This is true. She cannot argue with it. Her ears tilt forward — focused-posture, involuntary, the base of each pinna angling toward the source that has no physical source — and she lets them. She used to try to hold them still in public. She has mostly stopped. The ears do what the ears do. Learning to stop apologizing for the reflexes was, the integration counselor told her, part of the process. She is finding it is part of a larger process the integration counselor does not know about.

[Beat 454]
The Pulse passes the Belmont maintenance platform — not a stop, just the infrastructure showing itself briefly, a lit concrete shelf where a four-person crew is working on something in the coil housing. She can see them through the glass and hear them through it, the sound arriving a half-second before the visual resolves into faces: they're arguing about whose fault something is in the comfortable way of people who have been arguing the same argument for years and have stopped needing it to resolve. One of them gestures at something with a torque tool. Another one shrugs with his entire body. The pod moves on. Her ears hold the sound of their argument a moment longer than her eyes hold the sight of them, and then both let go, and the tunnel takes the pod again.

[Beat 455]
The Network hums in the raw channel — not the voice, just the field itself, the ambient weight of a connected city feeling its own load. She has learned in three weeks to read some of it: the data district south of the Loop, dense and warm; the flood-control grid's continuous heartbeat, a saturation she feels at the back of the jaw. Jace could navigate the whole of it from a single point of contact, reading it the way you read a room by how sound moves in it. She is not good at this yet. She is learning.

[Beat 456]
A woman across the car: new cat ears, white-tipped, still carrying the particular stiffness of ears that haven't finished learning their own weight. Maybe twenty-five, maybe younger, watching the tunnel wall with the careful intentional stillness of integration week two — the not-moving-to-prove-you-can-not-move phase, the one that lasts until you stop caring whether anyone is watching. Celeste's own ears swivel toward her before the decision forms. The woman's ears swivel back. Neither of them says anything. But they are the only two people in this car who heard the maintenance crew arguing at Belmont, who caught the dog near the Loyola platform, who felt the ventilation frequency shift in the tunnel at the curve. Something passes between them in that shared fact, brief and complete, the way a nod works when both parties know exactly what it means.

[Beat 457]
The stop. The platform surfaces into Logan Square and the neighborhood arrives in layers: sound first, then light, then the brick and the murals as the train slows. The ears catch the food cart before she sees it — a sealed-flame grill unit, the specific acoustic signature of fat hitting hot metal, a woman humming something without a fixed melody, the kind of hum that keeps you company rather than saying anything. Two blocks north, the standby hum of an Arcturus checkpoint reader cycling through its idle scan, no personnel present at this hour. She has the grill, the humming, the checkpoint in that order before she's stepped off the platform. She steps off. The night air is cooler here than Uptown, the brick holding a different cold than the lake-effect, older somehow, more decided.

[Beat 458]
She walked this neighborhood once, in middle school — a registered operator showed her class diagrams of the Auricula Felis structure on a lightboard, genemod licensing protocols, everything formal and documented. She was twelve and standing at the back of the room thinking: there must be a version of this that people do without the paperwork. She didn't know what she meant by that then. She knows now that she meant exactly this: two blocks east on Kedzie, at 03:08, with a bag and a name to say at an intercom.

[Beat 459]
A Behemoth on the corner — mid-size, civil-maintenance class, working a utility junction with the precise methodical attention of something that has no attention in any sense she means by the word. Its movements are mechanical and complete and entirely without awareness of her. She knows what it is. She knows what they are. She still tenses. The shoulders do it before she can talk them out of it.

The voice says, forward-left, warm and exact: “It's a fridge with legs, Cel.”

And she is sixteen again on the Davis Street platform, a maintenance unit trundling along the far edge and her hand finding Jace's sleeve, and Jace saying it just like that — not mocking her, never mocking, the words pitched low so only she got them, the small laugh underneath that told her he thought the fear was sweet and not stupid. She can feel the exact weight of his arm not pulling away. The warmth of it goes through her chest like heat through a wall.

Then the second thought arrives, quieter, underneath: she reached for that memory to make sure. She went looking for the texture of him saying it and there it was, complete, and the having of it told her the voice was him. She lets the thought pass without finishing it. She breathes out once, measured, and walks past the Behemoth. It does not turn. It finishes what it is doing with the junction cover and moves to the next panel. She doesn't look back.

[Beat 460]
The name and the word are for a door, and a door at 03:10 in a neighborhood she has never walked is a thing you find your way to, not a thing you arrive at. The voice had given her a landmark — the building with the rotating signage, the shared-light facade, north of Kedzie — but a landmark is not a route, and between her and it is a stretch she can already hear before she reaches it: voices layered close, a door cycling open and shut, music with the bass pulled out of it the way music gets when a room wants to talk over itself. A mod hub. She knows the sound of one without having been in one, the way you know the sound of a market before you turn the corner into it.

She tells herself she will walk past it. Then she tells herself the truth, which is that she has a name and a word and no map of the last hundred feet, that she needs to ask someone which building is which, and that a room full of people who do this without paperwork is exactly where you ask.

The voice is quiet. It has been quiet since the Behemoth. She notices the quiet and decides it means the voice trusts her to do this part herself.

[Beat 461]
The place has no sign she can read — just a converted ground-floor unit, the front windows papered over from the inside with old licensing notices and band flyers, the door propped with a fire extinguisher. Inside, the air is a wall: warm, close, the smell of sweat and ozone and the antiseptic tang of fresh work, somebody's dermal channels recently sealed and still off-gassing the curing agent. The light is low and uneven, run off a string of mismatched fixtures, and half of it isn't electric at all — it comes off people. A man at the bar with luminescent patches down both arms, a soft aquarium blue. A woman whose scalp is mapped in slow-pulsing green lines that follow the vascular pattern of her skull. Someone in the corner lit faintly gold from the inside of their own mouth when they laugh.

Her ears do not know where to point. That is the first thing. The room is too full — too many small sounds, too many sources, the bass she can't quite hear pressing on the back of her jaw, a dozen conversations and the surgical whine of a sealing tool from a back room and the hiss of a tap and someone counting Φ out loud in a language she catches the shape of but not the words. Both pinna sweep and reset and sweep again, hunting for a floor to stand on, and there isn't one. For three weeks the ears have given her the inventory before she asks. In here they give her static. She hadn't understood until this moment how much she'd come to lean on them. She stands just inside the door and makes herself small and breathes through the antiseptic and tries to find a person who looks like they answer questions.

[Beat 462]
A man finds her before she finds anyone. He is suddenly at her shoulder the way people are suddenly at your shoulder when they have practiced the approach — middle-aged, soft-handed, a lanyard with a laminated card she can't read in the light, the warm overhead manner of someone who has decided to be helpful at you.

"First time," he says. Not a question. His eyes go to the ears and stay there a half-second too long, the way the contact's hadn't. "Fresh, too. Three weeks? Four? You can always tell — they sit high before they learn to settle." He smiles. "You looking for placement? Aftercare? I run consults in the back. No charge to talk. Most operators in here'll fleece you on the calibration, charge you a thousand Φ to tune what you could tune yourself in a week. I just like to make sure the new ones don't get taken."

The words are correct. Every word is a word a helpful person would say. But the ears — even drowning, even with no floor under them — catch the one thing under the words: his heartbeat, close enough now to read, faster than his voice. A calm man with a fast heart. She hears the distance between what he says and what he is, and lets it sit. She didn't yet know what it meant. She knew it was there.

"I'm meeting someone," she says.

[Beat 463]
"Sit a minute. Get your bearings. You're swimming — I can see it, the ears doing that, that's overload, that's normal, it passes." He is already steering her, a hand not quite touching her back, toward a stool at the end of the bar where a drink is waiting that she did not order — a short glass, something pale, already poured, already there. The wrongness of it arrives a half-beat late: the drink was poured before she walked in, or poured for whoever he was working before her, or poured because this is the part where the drink is waiting. "On me. House does a hibiscus thing, it's good for the integration nerves, half these kids swear by it." His fast heart. The waiting glass. The half-second on her ears.

She does not sit. She has her hand flat on the bar to refuse the stool without making it a scene when a girl two stools down says, without looking up from her own glass, in a flat bored voice pitched exactly loud enough to reach Celeste and no further:

"Don't drink that."

[Beat 464]
The girl is maybe twenty, maybe a little more, dressed in black layers gone soft and gray at the seams from washing, a heavy braid threaded with dried something — small flowers, real ones, pressed and going brown. Down both forearms, under the skin, run channels of bioluminescence in the shape of fern fronds, fiddleheads curling at the wrist and unrolling toward the elbow, a low steady green that is nothing like the man's aquarium blue — older work, settled work, the glow dimmed to almost nothing the way a thing dims when its owner has stopped wanting to be looked at. At her temples, vine-pattern tattoos shift when she moves her jaw, the lines crawling a few millimeters and resettling. She has a split lip, recent, scabbed at one corner, and she does not seem to care about it.

"He poured it before you got here," the girl says, still to her own glass. "He pours two or three a night, leaves them sitting, walks the door. Whoever looks newest. Tonight you're newest." Now she does look up, and her eyes go to the cat ears with none of the man's appetite, just a quick professional read, the look of someone pricing a thing she has no intention of buying. "Three weeks. Fresh ears, nice ones, licensed-clinic work, which means money behind you, which means you came in from somewhere with money and you don't know anybody in here. That's the whole order he reads off you. It's not subtle. He's just counting on you not knowing the room."

The man's helpful warmth has gone somewhere behind his face. "Inés," he says, and her name comes out of him like a complaint he's filed before.

"Esteban," the girl says back, the exact same way, and does not get off her stool.

[Beat 465]
Inés's fern-channels dimmed before anything happened. Not after — before. One breath before the inner door moved, the low green channels in her forearms went to dark skin, all at once, the involuntary shutoff that Celeste would later understand was the botanical version of pinna flattening. She set her glass down. Then the door.

Esteban weighed it — the new girl who won't sit, the regular who won't shut up, a room that has started, in the way of rooms, to have an extra few faces turned their direction. He picked up the waiting glass himself, unbothered, like it was always his, and drifted back toward the propped door to wait for someone easier. That was all it was. No threat, no scene. A man who fishes a crowded pond and doesn't mind losing one.

Celeste let out a breath she had been holding without deciding to hold it. "Thank you," she said, and meant it more than the size of the words.

"Don't thank me yet, you're still standing in the worst place to stand," Inés said, and almost smiled, and that was when the door banged.

Not the propped door — the inner one, to the back rooms, kicked open by a boot. The man who came through it was built like the rooms behind him: a low square skull plated under the skin at the jaw and temple so the bone showed its true edges, chrome knuckle-housings on both hands catching the bad light, the whole of him arranged to be read as a wall. He was already talking, already loud, the specific volume of a man who has decided the room belongs to him because nothing in it can hurt him. He was looking for someone. The someone was the girl two stools down.

Inés's fern-channels were already dark. Had been, for one breath, before he arrived. Celeste hadn't understood yet why that mattered.

[Beat 466]
He has her by the braid before Celeste fully tracks the distance closing — a fist of chrome knuckles wound into the dried flowers and the hair, hauling her half off the stool, and he is saying her name and saying where were you and saying you don't get to just leave, the words running together under the volume. Inés gets a hand up, not to hit him, to cover, and that is the hand he goes through. The first blow is open, a slap that turns her head and stops the room's nearest three conversations. The second is not open. The chrome housings come down across her cheekbone with the flat heavy sound of metal meeting a person, and her face opens at the bone, and blood comes fast and dark, and the sound her ears give Celeste — the ears finding their floor at last, finding the one thing in the room loud and clear and singular enough to lock onto — is the small wet give of it, the specific sound of a face being hurt, arriving a half-instant before the visual, the way every important sound in this city has arrived for her tonight a half-instant early.

And the room looks away. That is the part Celeste will keep. Not all at once and not completely — a flinch, a turning of shoulders, the aquarium-blue man finding something fascinating in his own glass, the gold-mouthed laugher gone quiet and studying the floor. Forty people in a hot bright room and the math runs through all of them at once and comes out the same: chrome and plate, knuckle housings, a wall that picks fights because it has built itself unable to lose them. Nobody wants the second half of his evening. He winds the braid tighter and draws back the chrome hand again and Inés's fern-channels are dark and her covered face is bleeding and the room has decided, the way rooms decide, that this is happening to someone else.

[Beat 467]
Celeste does not decide to move so much as find that she has already moved — across the small distance, into the space the whole room has cleared around the two of them, into the light, where everyone can see her. She does not touch him. She is not strong and she knows it and she does not pretend otherwise. She puts herself between the chrome hand and the girl's broken face and she makes herself the most visible thing in the room, and she says, loud, pitched to carry to every turned-away shoulder in it:

"Two blocks north there's an Arcturus checkpoint reader running idle scan. It's logging." This is true. She heard it cycling from the platform, no personnel, just the standby hum of a machine keeping a record nobody is reading yet. "Your jaw plate has a license serial. So do the knuckle housings — registered cyberware throws an ID on any reader inside 130 feet whether it's manned or not. You hit her again in here and there are forty people who saw it and a machine two blocks away that already knows your serial was standing in this room at this minute. That's not a fight you win by being hard to hurt."

She does not know if all of it is exactly true. She knows enough of it is. She has spent three weeks learning what the readers resolve at distance and she is betting, out loud, in the light, that a man who built himself unable to lose a fight has never once thought about the kind of loss that doesn't come through his fists. Her own ears are flat to her skull, scared flat, both pinna tight and pointing back, and she does not try to lift them. She lets him see them flat. She lets him see she is afraid and standing there anyway. That is the thing she has that the room doesn't: she will let herself be seen wanting this to stop.

[Beat 468]
The room comes back. That is what does it — not her words exactly, but the fact that someone said words at all, that the contract of looking-away has been broken by one person standing in the light, and the breaking gives the others permission. The bartender, who has watched the whole thing with the stillness of someone who watches it often, finally puts both hands flat on the bar and says his name — Dario — in the tired voice of a man invoking a rule. The aquarium-blue man turns back around. The gold-mouthed one stands up. Forty people remembering, all at once, that forty is a number, that a wall is only a wall to one set of hands.

Dario — the chrome man, she has his name now and marks it — does the math a second time and gets a different answer. He calls her a word. He calls Inés several. He lets go of the braid with a shove that puts her into the bar, and he points the chrome hand at Celeste's face, close, the housings cold-smelling, and tells her she has no idea whose night she just got into. Then he goes, because going is the only move left that lets him keep the story that he is a wall. The propped door bangs. The fire extinguisher skitters. The room exhales as one body and goes back, mostly, to its drinks, because that is also what rooms do.

Celeste's hands are shaking. She notices this clinically, from a small distance, the way she notices most things. She has made an enemy in a city she has been inside of for under an hour. That is the cost, and she paid it on purpose, and she would pay it again, and she takes note of that about herself too.

[Beat 469]
She gets Inés into the back, to a sink with actual running water, because the back rooms are where the work happens and the work needs water. Inés lets her, which Celeste understands is its own kind of trust — letting a stranger near a face that is already broken. The cut runs along the cheekbone, deep, the chrome having split skin against bone, and it will scar, and there is nothing in Celeste's bag or her three weeks of training that fixes that, so she does the thing she can do: she holds a folded cloth to it with steady pressure and she keeps her own ears, finally, doing the one useful thing they have managed all night, which is listening to the room behind them for the door, in case Dario is the kind to come back.

"You could've not done that," Inés says, through the cloth, not quite a thank-you, more like a fact she is turning over. Her fern-channels have come back up, faint, a low green pulse Celeste can see at the edges of the rolled sleeves. "Most people don't. I've watched most people not, in this exact room. He's done this in here before." A pause. "You're not from here at all, are you. You walked in tonight."

"Tonight," Celeste says.

"And you went at Dario your first hour." Inés laughs, which hurts her, which she does anyway. "You're going to die very fast in this neighborhood if somebody doesn't walk you around it once." She takes the cloth from Celeste's hand and holds it herself and looks at her, direct, the read different now, no longer pricing — appraising something she has decided she likes. "Where are you trying to get to?"

[Beat 470]
Celeste hesitates — the name and the word are not things you say in a bar — and into the hesitation the voice comes, forward-left, warm, faster than it has come all night, faster than it came at the Behemoth, arriving the instant a second person has asked to be let into her night.

"She's good, Cel. You can feel it. She's exactly someone you'd have liked." A beat. "Tell her where. She'll get you there safe — safer than wandering. She found you for a reason."

And it is so precisely the thing she wants to hear that she says, "A building north of Kedzie, rotating sign, shared-light front," before she has finished weighing whether to. The voice is right. The voice is always right about what she wants, which is the thing about the voice. She does not, in the warmth of being told her new friend is good, notice how quickly the warmth arrived, how it did not wait to know Inés at all, how eight months of a voice that has been her only company did not so much as pause before approving the first person in eight months to stand between her and harm.

Inés's vine-tattoos shift at her temples. "The collective. Yeah, I know it — quiet operator, runs people through, doesn't ask the wrong questions. I can get you to the door. Five minutes if you don't get lost, and you'd get lost." She straightens, winces, presses the cloth. "Okay. Yeah. Let's get you off the street before Dario decides to wait outside."

[Beat 471]
Inés walks her through the neighborhood the way the contact will later walk her through the chain — not reciting, just the worn shorthand of someone who has lived a geography long enough that it has gone smooth in her mouth. This block is fine, this one isn't after midnight. The clinic with the green cross painted small is real and licensed and overcharges; the one two doors down with no sign does better work for half but you go with someone the first time. Esteban works three bars on a rotation; she gives Celeste the rotation. Dario runs with a plate-crew out of a garage off Cermak and the smart move is to be somewhere he isn't for a week. The Behemoths reroute foot traffic when they're working a junction, so if a block is suddenly empty it's the machine, not a reason to be scared, and if a block is suddenly empty and the machine isn't working it, be scared.

Celeste listens the way she has listened to everything tonight, cataloguing, and finds — for the first time since the platform — that she is cataloguing a person, not a threat. The ears are calm. They sit in their lazy outward idle, the relaxed posture, swiveling now and then toward Inés's voice because the voice is the most interesting thing on the street and not because anything is wrong. She had not had this since Jace: someone walking beside her who knows more than she does and is spending it on her without being asked. She is careful not to let the size of that show on her face.

[Beat 472]
They stop across the street from the building with the rotating sign. Inés does not cross. "This is you. I don't go in places I'm not asked into — the operator here's careful, and careful's why he's still doing this." She presses the cloth to her cheek one more time and takes it away and looks at the dark stain on it and folds it to a clean side with the unbothered competence of someone who has folded a lot of bloody cloth. "If you need to find me. There's a noodle counter on Milwaukee, the one with the broken sign, the H's out so it just says 'OUSE.' I'm there most mornings, late morning, I do my eating when the night people are asleep and the day people are at work and the place is empty. Ask for Inés. They know me." She says her own name plainly, a gift handed over without ceremony. "What do I call you?"

Celeste weighs it for exactly one second — the habit of the night, the name and the word are not things you say — and then doesn't, because this is not the bar and this is not a lever someone is working and this is a person who put her own face between Celeste and a bad drink for free.

"Celeste."

"Celeste." Inés tries it once, settles it. "Okay, Celeste. Don't drink anything you didn't watch get poured. Don't go at Dario again, I mean it, once was lucky." A pause, and the almost-smile, the real one, careful around the split lip. "Thanks for being stupid back there. It's nice when somebody's stupid in the right direction." Then she is gone up Milwaukee, fern-light low and green at her wrists, a small bright botanical thing moving off into a dark street that does not, Celeste understands, scare her at all.

[Beat 473]
The building looks exactly like what it is trying to look like: a residential collective, four stories, the rotating signage cycling through tenant-board announcements that are two weeks out of date. The shared-light facade is on its night setting, amber-dim. Second floor, one window lit. She stands on the sidewalk a moment after Inés has gone, alone again but differently — not lost now, delivered, the last hundred feet that the voice could not give her handed over by a stranger with a broken face. The ears inventory the building the way they could not inventory the bar: a television on the third floor, the particular sound of someone who has fallen asleep to it; water in the pipes, the small random percussion of an old building doing its work; and from the second floor, nothing yet — just the faint creak of a chair's weight, someone sitting still, waiting. The contact is awake. The contact is expecting someone. She has the name and the word. She steps to the intercom and uses them.

[Beat 474]
The intercom carries her name and the word back to her own ears as a thin tinny double, and then there's the second-floor chair taking its weight off — the creak releasing rather than settling — and a floorboard, and another. A pause while the building decides about her: four seconds, five. Then the lock throws somewhere inside the frame, a heavy mechanical clunk she feels in her teeth, and the door opens.

[Beat 475]
She hears him before the second-floor landing: his footsteps have the weight-forward gait of someone carrying too much in the front of his coat, a slight compensation on each step that the ears read as habit rather than injury. He opens the door before she knocks. He's in his forties, broad through the shoulders, a face that was probably once expressive and has since decided against it. He looks at her: the ears first — and for one beat, before his face closes back over, something moves in it, the reflex of a person who has seen this before and has not managed to stop noticing it — then the bag, then her face. She watches him weigh what he was expecting against what arrived, and adjust, and step back, and hold the door open. He doesn't say her name. He doesn't say any name. That is correct procedure. She steps inside.

[Beat 476]
He sits her at a table cleared of everything except a paper notebook and a stylus, the kind of analog setup that registers to nothing and exists nowhere after the pages are gone. She clocks that before she sits: no terminal, no glass, nothing in the room that could be listening, and she puts her own bag down on the side of the chair away from the door, where she'd have to step over it to be rushed. Small things. Things she'd worked out on the train, sitting in the window seat with her eyes closed, deciding in advance what a safe room would have to look like before she'd let her shoulders down in it. She lets them down a quarter of the way.

He walks her through it in the order she needs it: the chain is long, she'll be moved twice more, the safe flat in Pilsen is where she'll stay through integration, the operator there is vetted and quiet and has done this before. He talks about it the way someone talks about a route they have driven many times — not reciting, not performing calm, just the shorthand of repetition, the compressed language of a person who has explained the same geography often enough that the words have worn smooth. She tracks the shorthand for the seams a liar leaves and doesn't find them. That, more than anything he says, is what loosens the next quarter.

[Beat 477]
She asks whether other people do this.

The question comes out more plainly than she'd planned — the careful version of herself would have held it back, would have waited until she understood the room better. But she is sitting at a table in a building she found through a chain of names she memorized and did not write down, with a man whose name she does not know and who has not offered it, and she wants to know what she is.

Not whether it is safe. She has thought about safe and made her peace with it. Just whether others have sat at this table, carried a voice in the same direction she has been carrying one, asked a version of the same question.

[Beat 478]
He says yes, without looking up from the notebook page he's been setting up, and the flatness of it — the yes as simple fact, not reassurance — is more steadying than reassurance would have been.

[Beat 479]
She asks: "Are they okay?"

He says: "They get what they came for." He says it without looking up, in the tone of something that is true and that he has thought about enough to stop questioning. She hears the gap — what she asked and what he answered are not quite the same shape — and it sits in her chest for a moment before the voice comes, forward-left, patient and certain: "You never had to explain that question to me, Cel. He doesn't know what you're asking. Nobody else ever did." She holds the gap for one more second and then lets it go. She chooses to believe it.

[Beat 480]
He asks how long she's been hearing the voice. She says eight months. He nods once, the nod of a man confirming a data point, and opens the paper notebook and begins to write with the stylus — actual paper, a drag-and-catch resistance she can hear in the room's quiet, each letter a small physical cost. The low light makes reading difficult, but she can make out one word upside-down through the angle and the shadow: *early.*

Her ears flatten, fast, both pinna tight against her skull, tips pointing back — the scared flat, the reflex that means something in the auditory architecture has registered a threat before she has.

She almost asks. She has her mouth open and the question formed — *what do you mean, early* — and she closes it, because she understands what asking would mean. Asking would mean she is someone who doesn't already know. Asking would mean she is here under false pretenses, that the eight months she has built toward this is not what she has been building. She presses her left ear flat the rest of the way with her palm and waits.

The voice says nothing. Not a word, not even the warmth of its presence — just a silence where it always is, where for eight months it has always been.

She waits a moment longer. The contact's stylus drags and catches. The room holds its low cold light. The voice does not come.

She takes her hand down and doesn't ask the question. The contact keeps writing.

[Beat 481]
He finishes. He closes the notebook. He says she can stay tonight and will be moved in the morning, early. She says thank you. He stands, and the chair makes the sound of a man who has stood from it many times — a creak worn into the wood, specific and habitual. He doesn't say she's welcome. He goes to the kitchen. The tap runs, brief, and something in a cabinet settles on a shelf, and the kitchen light goes out.

The apartment is quieter. She sits at the table with her hands flat on its surface and the closed notebook in front of her. She doesn't open it. It holds nothing back, which is the only kind of honest the situation allows.

She stays at the table until the kitchen's absence has settled into the room. Then she gets up and goes to find the spare room.

[Beat 482]
The spare room is at the back of the building, a cot against one wall and a window looking onto the alley and the service lights of the Cermak corridor blinking in the middle distance. Someone has left a plant on the windowsill — small, three leaves, ceramic pot with a chip on the rim, one leaf gone yellow at the tip but the other two holding their green with the particular stubbornness of something that has decided to stay alive despite the light situation. She doesn't know what kind it is. She stands in the doorway for a moment and looks at it, and then she steps inside.

[Beat 483]
She puts the bag on the floor beside the cot and sits without unpacking. The bag on the floor is different from the bag on the bed: the bag on the bed was preparation, rehearsal, the future in physical form. The bag on the floor is a thing she has brought somewhere. She is somewhere. She sits with her hands on her knees and doesn't do anything for a while, and the room holds this without comment — someone else's spare room at 03:20, the alley sounds coming through the glass at low volume, the service lights doing their slow patient work in the corridor.

[Beat 484]
The voice doesn't come for a few minutes. It lets the room be what it is - lets the delivery drone's route-calculation ping filter through the glass, the Rider somewhere across the city with their body left behind in a chair while the drone's sensors become their eyes, lets the water in the pipe below her do its slow work, lets the service-light hum from the Cermak corridor fill the space between her breaths. She doesn't reach for it. She has learned that reaching closes the channel slightly, that waiting opens it, some mechanic of attention she doesn't fully understand yet. Then: "You're doing it right, Cel." Warm. Unhurried. Certain in the way that only certainty earned across eight months can be certain. She exhales and her shoulders drop a half-inch.

[Beat 485]
She lies down on the cot without taking her jacket off. The fabric of the jacket is something she can locate — the seam at her shoulder, the weight of the collar — and she needs that right now, the jacket as perimeter. The ceiling is low and water-stained in one corner in a shape that is not a shape, just damage expressing itself over time. The service lights from the Cermak corridor move across it every few seconds. She watches them.

*He wrote* early *and the voice went quiet and she let the question close.*

She knows what it means that she let it close. She has always known, when she is honest, that the eight months have been eight months of building a story she could live inside — Jace's voice, Jace's particular warmth, the way he said her name with the soft l, Cel not Celeste, the way no one else has ever shortened it quite that way. She has built it carefully and with everything she had. She has not asked the question at the center of it because the question at the center of it has teeth.

*If it's not him it's still the only thing that stayed.*

The voice comes again, quiet, no words this time — just presence, the forward-left warmth she has mapped so precisely across eight months that she could draw it in space with her finger. Her ears lift slightly off her skull, the involuntary adjustment, the auricula felis tracking something it has decided matters. She doesn't reach for the voice. She waits. It stays. She puts her hand flat on her sternum and feels her own breathing and the ceiling goes on doing its slow work with the light.

[Beat 486]
She is, for the first time in eight months, not in her parents' house. The thought arrives plain and dry and doesn't scare her. It is the first thing in eight months that hasn't. She holds it carefully — the way you hold something that might change if you examine it too directly, something that requires peripheral attention rather than the full weight of looking — and she waits for morning, and the plant on the windowsill catches a slant of corridor light across its two good leaves, and the ears track something in the alley below, and she lets them, and she breathes, and the city keeps going.

## What She Asked For

[Beat 487]
She wakes before she opens her eyes. The ears are already working — they tracked into consciousness a beat or two ahead of her, and what they brought back is a street market starting up below. The percussion of it is specific: a cart frame dropped onto pavement and adjusted twice, a vendor arguing cheerfully with a neighbor about placement, the back-and-forth in rapid Spanish where the anger is performative and both of them know it. And underneath all that, threading through the gap in the window frame that she found last night with her fingers, the smell of something sweet-savory on sealed flame. She lies still and lets it arrive. She doesn't open her eyes.

[Beat 488]
She opens her eyes.

The ceiling is the same water stain, the same low light coming off the alley, but the service lights from the Cermak corridor have changed their rhythm — morning cycle, slower, the maintenance pulse instead of the routing pulse, and she knows this because she spent the night learning the difference without meaning to. The plant on the windowsill is a different green in the morning. The chipped ceramic pot holds a thin line of shadow along its rim that wasn't there before.

*Jace never woke up early. He slept like something that had decided sleeping was the correct activity and was committed to it.*

She sits up. Her jacket is still on. She doesn't take it off now either — there's no reason to, and the seam at her shoulder is still doing its work, still where it was, the collar still its exact weight. She puts her feet on the floor and her hands on her knees and she is in a stranger's spare room at whatever hour the street market below says it is, which is early, which is the hour she asked to be moved, which means the contact is probably already awake in the kitchen and the tap has probably already run. She listens. It has. The apartment is doing its quiet morning version of itself.

*The notebook is on the table. She didn't bring it in here. She left it closed on the table and she is going to have to pick it up.*

She doesn't reach for the voice. She waits, as she has learned to. It comes — the forward-left warmth, present and unhurried, not speaking, just there. Her ears adjust by a fraction. She breathes. The vendor below lands the punchline of his argument and his neighbor laughs and the cart frame settles into its final position with a sound of metal finding metal, and she stands up, and picks up the bag from the floor, and goes to get the notebook.

[Beat 489]
The voice, forward-left, easy: "Morning, Cel." Just that. Not a briefing, not guidance. Just the word. She rolls onto her side toward it — toward the wall on the left, toward nothing — and the ears tilt up, involuntary, base lifting, tracking the direction where there is no direction to track. She lets them. She has stopped fighting that particular reflex.

[Beat 490]
The voice was starting to give her the day — the community board on 18th and Halsted, it was saying, start there — when the knock came. Three taps on the door, unhurried, a knuckle that knew the door was thin and didn't need to hit it hard. The voice stopped. The ears swung off the forward-left and pinned the sound to the hallway, to a person standing close enough that Celeste could hear the small shift of weight from one foot to the other, the brush of soft fabric, breathing that was steady and not at all worried about being heard. She was off the cot and against the wall beside the door before she'd thought about it. She hadn't told anyone this address. She had walked herself here last night by a route she'd taken twice to be sure of it.

"It's Inés," the voice on the other side said. Not the forward-left voice. A real voice, a little rough, late-morning low. "From the bar. You gave me the building, you didn't think I'd find the floor?" A pause. "Open the door, I brought breakfast and it's getting cold standing here."

[Beat 491]
Celeste opened the door. Inés was leaning on the frame the way a person leans when they've decided a place is theirs to lean on — black layers gone soft at the seams, the heavy braid with its dried flowers gone browner than two nights ago, a paper bag in one hand giving off steam through a torn corner. The cut on her cheekbone from Dario's chrome knuckles had closed to a dark seam and would scar; she plainly didn't care. Down both forearms the fern channels ran their low settled green, fiddleheads curling at the wrists, the light steady. She looked Celeste over once — the ears first, then the rest — the way you check that a thing you left somewhere is still where you left it.

"Huh," Inés said. "You slept. Good. Most people in your week don't." She held out the bag. "Get dressed. I'm not eating in a hallway and I'm not eating in your room, it smells like somebody died of being twenty-two in here. There's a counter on Milwaukee. You met me, now you meet the rest of it." She said it like the matter was already settled, which, Celeste understood, it was.

[Beat 492]
She washed her face fast in the flat's cold water while Inés waited in the hall, and the voice came once, low, forward-left, while the water was running. "She's the one from the bar." Not a question — it had been there for the bar, the way it was there for everything. "Be careful what you tell her, Cel. People like her, they've decided how the world works. They'll fit you into it whether you fit or not." It wasn't a warning against Inés exactly. It was gentler than that, sorrier than that, the tone of someone who'd watched her get hurt before and didn't want to watch it again. She dried her face on the corner of the towel. She didn't answer the voice, and she noticed, briefly, that she didn't — that for the first time in a long time there was a person on the other side of a door, expecting her, and the voice was the thing she was keeping waiting.

[Beat 493]
The street was exactly as loud as she'd imagined from the bed, which meant it was not enough. The ears tracked vendors at five different distances at once, shoppers negotiating in English and Spanish and at least one language she didn't know, a Behemoth service unit working the east edge — she heard its weight-distribution shifts before she saw it emerge between two stalls, the pneumatic pause-and-reset of its legs on uneven pavement. Three vendors called at the same moment in three directions and the ears swiveled to catch all three in sequence, a half-second sweep that left her briefly dizzy. Inés walked through it without flinching, a half-step ahead, reading the crowd a different way — by faces, by who was where, by which storefronts had their lights on. "You're hearing all of it," she said, not turning around. "Don't. You'll burn yourself out by noon. You pick the one thing that matters and you let the rest be weather." She said it the way you'd tell someone to watch the step they were about to miss.

[Beat 494]
The noodle counter was on Milwaukee under a sign with two letters burned dark, so that what stayed lit said OUSE and nothing else. Late morning, the way Inés had described it two nights ago — the night people asleep, the day people at work, the place all but empty, six stools and a flat steel counter and a cook who lifted his chin at Inés and started a second bowl without being asked. The acoustic of it was small and close and Celeste's ears settled almost gratefully, the field of the street narrowing to the hiss of the flat-top, the cook's spatula, water knocking in a pipe behind the wall. Inés took the corner stool, the one with its back to the wall and a sightline to the door, and waited for Celeste to notice it was the only stool she'd ever take and to take the one beside it.

[Beat 495]
A woman had passed them on the walk over — tall, moving with a particular ease, with elaborate fin-structured ear mods that weren't cat-based, something more like deep-water fish, silver-edged and mobile, the fins fanned and tracking a sound down the street before the woman's eyes moved. Celeste's ears had swiveled in the same direction before she could stop them, both of them pulled toward the same point by the same signal. The woman had glanced at her — a brief recognition, the look between people who've done the same kind of work to themselves — and gone on into the crowd. Now, on the stool, Inés had caught the whole thing. "You feel that?" she asked. "Her and you, same second, same direction. That's the part nobody warns you about. You're not just hearing more. You're hearing it with everybody else who hears more. It's a city inside the city." She said it plainly, no awe in it. It was just true, and she'd lived in it long enough that it had stopped being remarkable to her and was only remarkable to watch land on someone new.

[Beat 496]
The bowls came. Inés ate the way she did everything, without ceremony, watching Celeste over the steam. "So," she said, when she'd decided enough time had passed. "Licensed-clinic ears, three weeks, money behind you, and you're down here knocking on basement doors instead of going back to whoever paid for these." She tipped her chopsticks at Celeste's head. "Nobody does that for fun. People do that when they want something the clean shops won't sell them, and there's only a few things on that list." She let it sit. Celeste's ears had drawn back a degree — not flat, just wary, the involuntary half-step of a thing that's been seen. Inés caught it and didn't push. "I'm not asking what it is. I'm telling you I already know the shape of it, so don't waste either of our mornings pretending it's something else. You're chasing a person. That's the only thing that gets a kid your age down here with that look." She went back to her bowl. "Eat. We've got a lot of street to cover and you walk slow."

[Beat 497]
Inés walked her to the community board on the corner of 18th and Halsted — physical, paper, tacked in layers, announcements and offerings and warnings palimpsested over each other back a year or more, the older ones faded to near-white. "Digital postings, somebody can edit those, take them down, pretend they never said it," Inés said. "This stays. You burn somebody on this board, the board remembers." She found the recommendation section without looking, the way you find a thing in your own kitchen. Celeste already had a name from a contact in Logan Square — Ines, spelled the formal way, Vásquez — and she wanted to see it appear here independently, uncoupled from the first chain. It did. Three separate mentions, three different hands. One said: *She'll tell you things you don't want to hear and she's right every time.* Inés tapped that one. "Her. If you can get her to take you, take her. Don't haggle. People who can hear what's wrong with you are worth their rate." Celeste committed it to memory without deciding to.

[Beat 498]
There was a storefront a block north with a clean facade, and the voice came forward-left while they were still at the board — quiet, certain, the way it gave her things. "That one, Cel. The clean one. He'll do the work." She had started toward it before she'd weighed it, the way she'd been starting toward things the voice named for eight months. Inés put two fingers on her sleeve, not gripping, just landing there, and Celeste stopped. "Not him," Inés said. "He's licensed clean, which means he reports clean. You walk in there, your name's in a database by dinner." Which was exactly the thing Celeste could not afford — her father monitored those — and the voice had pointed her straight at it, warm and sure. She felt the small wrongness of that and set it aside, the way she set most small wrongnesses aside lately. "How do you know which way to go?" she asked. Inés glanced at her — at the half-second tilt of Celeste's head toward the empty air on her left, the listening she did before she moved, which Inés had clocked twice now and said nothing about.
